#7326. Senior Growth Marketing Manager (App Installs) at DuckDuckGo

Hi, we're DuckDuckGo, the online protection company and remote-first team of 400+ on a mission to raise the standard of trust online. Founded in 2008 and profitable since 2014, our annual revenue is in the hundreds of millions USD and millions use our browser on Mac, Windows, iOS, and Android, our search engine, and the DuckDuckGo subscription. We also offer private, useful, and optional AI, including Duck.ai, which lets you chat privately with ChatGPT, Claude, and other AIs, all in one place.

Our culture of trust, inclusivity, and empowered project management underpins everything we do, where each team member takes full ownership of their projects, from scoping and execution to postmortem. If you're seeking end-to-end ownership of your work, you've come to the right place!

Working on the Marketing & Communications Functional Team, you'll manage paid media channels to drive profitable acquisition of new app users, aim to help millions of people take back control of their personal information, and work on related projects. Recent projects include:

Scaling and optimizing app acquisition campaigns in Google, Microsoft and Apple ad platforms

Launching new ad channel tests and measuring incremental lift

Executing in-market tests for new creative and messaging to improve app install conversion

7+ years of experience in digital advertising roles with 2+ years of experience in marketing consumer apps preferred.

Hands-on expertise standing up and managing paid digital advertising programs (Google Ads, Apple Ads, etc.) as well as familiarity with non-digital paid media management (CTV, TV, etc.).

Demonstrated track record of profitably scaling app install growth programs while improving efficiency and unit economics as volume materially scales.

Ability to execute highly complex and ambiguous projects, working across business strategy and marketing tactics.

Experience designing and running incrementality tests (geo holdouts, lift studies, PSA/ghost ads) to validate true channel impact and guide investment decisions.

Advanced analytical abilities, including modeling app economics (payback, LTV), data analysis, scenario planning, and projecting campaign outcomes across spend and volume curves.

Track record of running creative testing as an always on pipeline, consistently shipping new concepts to prevent performance decay and unlock incremental scale.

Passion for ideating and problem solving to deliver creative solutions for profitable user growth.

Excellent communication skills — you can clearly articulate recommendations and rationale, whether verbal or written.

$178,500 USD annually and stock options. Compensation is transparent across the organization, and all team members within the same professional level and global region receive the same compensation.

Eligibility for company-sponsored health benefits is limited to team members based in the United States. This program does not extend to team members located in other countries, such as Canada or the UK.

#7325. Your Schema Markup Has a New Job. Is It Ready? via @sejournal, @marthavanberkel

AI is changing how customers discover and evaluate brands.

Enterprises need to think beyond rich results and develop the business context they publish for machines.

In 2027, schema markup has to be upgraded in order to maintain visibility in AI SERPs.

Get a free, personalized AI-Ready Schema Markup Scorecard, Now →

The new job I see for schema markup is to help AI understand your business, not just make individual pages eligible for rich results. That means explicitly describing your products, services, people, and locations, showing how they connect, and keeping those facts and relationships accurate as your business changes.

One of the biggest questions I hear from enterprise marketing leaders today is: How do we make sure AI is getting our brand right? It’s a fair question.

AI is increasingly influencing how customers discover, research, and choose businesses. Unlike traditional search, where customers could visit your website and form their own understanding, AI now brings information together from across the web to create answers on their behalf.

That information might come from your website, a third-party directory, customer reviews, or an article published years ago. And not all of it is necessarily accurate, complete, or up to date.

Here’s what I think is important for marketers to recognize: You can’t control everything AI says about your business. But you can take greater control of the information you give it, and that starts with your website.

Your website is one of the most valuable sources of information you own. It’s where you define who you are, what you offer, the expertise you bring to market, and what you want your business to be known for.

This is why I believe preparing the information you own for AI is one of the most important investments enterprise marketers can make today.

And if your organization already has schema markup, you’ve already started building that foundation. The opportunity now is to think bigger about what it can do, and make sure your strategy is built for the job schema markup needs to do today and tomorrow, not just the one it did yesterday.

The good news is that if you’ve already invested in schema markup, you’re not starting from scratch. But not all schema markup is created equal. Before you focus on doing more, ask whether what you already have gives machines a clear, connected understanding of your business.

The opportunity is to assess the foundation you’ve built, identify where it could be stronger, and build from there.

Here are three questions I’d encourage enterprise marketing leaders to ask.

Pro Tip: Use a AI-Ready Schema Markup Scorecard to assess your current foundation and identify opportunities to strengthen how machines understand your business.

Having schema markup across your website is a great start. But is it capturing the information that actually matters to your business?

Think about your most important products, services, locations, and areas of expertise. Does your markup describe them accurately and in enough detail? Or is it simply meeting the minimum requirements for a search feature? The goal isn’t just more schema markup; it’s more meaningful markup.

It’s one thing for machines to recognize the individual parts of your business. It’s another for them to understand how everything fits together. Are your people, products, services, and locations consistently identified and connected? Can machines recognize when different pages are talking about the same thing?

Think about a healthcare system. It might have schema markup describing its physicians, hospitals, and medical services. That’s a great start. But does that information clearly communicate which physicians specialize in which treatments? Where they practice? Which services are available at each location? And how all of those things connect back to the healthcare system?

Move beyond describing individual pages to communicating a connected picture of your business.

Your business is constantly evolving. New services, new locations, new expertise. And as your website content changes, your schema markup needs to keep up. Because it’s not enough for your website to tell one story to customers while your structured data tells another to machines.

As your website evolves, does your schema markup keep pace, reflecting the same accurate information and maintaining the relationships that connect your business?

Our Wells Fargo case study illustrates why this matters. Wells Fargo encountered an AI Overview that incorrectly identified a branch as permanently closed. By strengthening the structured information on its website, the team helped address that inaccurate answer.

You can’t control every answer AI generates. But you can take greater control of the accuracy and consistency of the information you’re giving it to work with. And that requires governance to be part of your approach from the beginning.

We need to think differently about what we’re asking our schema markup to accomplish. We should question whether we’re giving machines the information and context they need to understand our business as a whole, and recognize our relevance to the topics that matter most.

But here’s the challenge: Across large enterprise websites, that information is often spread across thousands of pages, managed by different teams, and constantly changing. Even when the information is accurate on individual pages, it doesn’t always tell a clear, connected story about the business.

And sometimes, we’re contributing to the confusion ourselves. As Schema App’s CTO Mark van Berkel discusses in Forbes, AI doesn’t always need to hallucinate incorrect information. Sometimes, it works with conflicting information businesses have already published.

If we want AI to understand our business, we need to give it more than individual pieces of information. We need to help it understand how those pieces fit together. And for large enterprises, that’s not a small challenge.

Think about everything your organization publishes across its website. Your products and services, locations, your people and their expertise, the topics you want to be known for. All of that information helps tell the story of your business. But across thousands of pages, different teams, and constantly changing content, those connections aren’t always clear.

What if we started thinking about our website not just as a collection of pages, but as a connected source of truth about our business? That’s the opportunity I see with a Marketing Context Layer.

A Marketing Context Layer is a governed foundation of information about your business that helps machines understand who you are, what you offer, what you’re known for, and how it all connects. It starts with the information you own and publish on your website.

Through advanced schema markup, you can make important business facts and relationships explicit, connecting them into a Content Knowledge Graph that can be maintained as your business evolves.

And this isn’t just a marketing conversation. Forrester has been exploring the importance of context layers in enterprise AI, recognizing the role of connected business knowledge in helping AI systems interpret and use information. I believe there’s an important opportunity to apply that same thinking to marketing. If enterprises are investing in governed business context to power AI internally, shouldn’t we also be thinking about the context we’re publishing for AI to understand us externally?

This is where schema markup becomes much more than an SEO tactic. It’s the starting point for building a connected, governed foundation of information about your business. One you can keep improving, maintaining, and putting to work as search and AI experiences evolve.

As our CTO, Mark van Berkel, explores in Forbes, the organizations that want to make meaningful use of AI need to invest in the information foundations that support it.

I believe we’re at an exciting turning point for enterprise marketers. We’ve spent years investing in our websites, creating content, and building our presence in search. Now we have an opportunity to make those investments work harder by helping machines understand the business behind the content.

And as AI continues to evolve, from search and answers to emerging agentic experiences, having clear, connected, and reliable business information will only become more important.

Because the goal isn’t just to be found. It’s to be understood. And that starts with taking ownership of the information we control.

You may already have schema markup. But how well is it communicating the full story of your business?

Our AI-Ready Schema Markup Scorecard helps you evaluate your existing implementation across key areas, identify gaps, and discover opportunities to build a stronger foundation for AI.

Get your personalized scorecard and explore your results in a 20-minute session with our team.

The opinions expressed in this article are the sponsor's own.

#7323. Instagram launches iPhone Duo-native app

Let Social Media Today's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The version includes functions specifically designed to take advantage of the device’s larger screen.

Instagram has launched a dedicated iPhone Duo version of its app that uses the device’s expanded display capacity to enable more on-screen functions.

As shown in this example image, the main Instagram feed will open to a wider presentation format, with more room on the screen.

For Reels, the Duo version of IG will display posts and comments in a side-by-side view, which will make it easier to view both elements.

As explained by Instagram: “When your phone is open, you can read comments beside a full-size Reel. The video keeps playing while you read along, so you can follow the conversation without shrinking what you’re watching.”

Instagram added that it’s moved the control buttons to the sidebar, which will leave more space for photos and videos.

“When your phone is folded, Instagram Stories and Reels keep their familiar vertical format,” Instagram said.

Messages and notifications will open into a two-column layout, with the inbox display on one side and highlighted exchanges on the other.

The custom presentation format could provide new opportunities for engagement and interaction in the app by making it easier for users to view content and see the related comments on a single screen.

That could also provide some interesting content considerations for creators, although this display format is restricted to Duo users only, which is a limiting factor.

Either way, this is a new view for Instagram, which aligns with Apple’s latest display approach.

Instagram also launched a Duo version of its Edits app last week.

#7319. Meta expands content protection to Instagram

Let Social Media Today's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The company is expanding its Facebook content protection systems, which use automatic detection features and alert creators when people repost their content.

Meta is expanding its content protection tools to Instagram, which will alert creators when other users repost their content, and enable them to take action on IP violations.

Meta added content protection on Facebook last year, which enables creators to activate protections for their original posts. Meta’s system then scans uploads as they come in, and alerts users to any copies of their content.

Now, the feature is coming to Instagram, with eligible creators able to enroll and protect their future uploads.

As per Instagram: “For eligible creators who enroll, content protection is a tool that automatically looks out for visual matches of your original videos, alerts you when we find a match across Instagram and Facebook, and lets you decide what happens next.”

That could offer peace of mind for creators who often see their content copied by aggregators and spammers.

Though it will be interesting to see how the system handles artificial intelligence-generated copies, which have become a much bigger issue of late.

With generative AI, users can easily create a replicant, or re-skinned version, of an original video in a completely different setting, or with completely different characters. As highlighted by the BBC News, one of the most popular examples of this trend has seen users creating alternate versions of the “Hotel Lobby” video by musicians Quavo and Takeoff.

Last month, Instagram Chief Adam Mosseri said that many creators have raised this trend as a concern, and added that he was also “very worried” about it.

Though he said that taking action will be tricky.

As Mosseri posted on Threads: “We are actively working out what to do, it’s just a tricky problem. We already let people make copyright claims against content, and take stolen copyrighted material. That’s not enough though, as assume many comedians and dancers haven’t gone through the trouble to copyright their material. We need a way to help those creatives without letting bad actors make more fake claims (happens all the time unfortunately).”

Instagram is experimenting with the addition of transcript detection for this exact purpose, which will alert creators when it detects a video that matches the spoken words in their content, even if the visuals have been altered by AI.

Instagram said that it’s testing this element with a small group of creators.

AI replicants will likely become a bigger problem moving forward, as more viral videos are re-framed, re-cast and re-created. Maybe, through expanded reporting, and new tactics like transcript detection, as well as the resulting copyright claims from those processes, Meta will be able to gain more training data to enhance its detection systems.

Though it’s unclear how keen Meta will actually be keen to crack down on AI use, given that its business has gone all-in on AI.

#7317. GrowthLoop: Partner Marketing Manager

As our Partner Marketing Manager, you’ll own marketing to, with, and through our partners, building on an established partner strategy. You’ll work closely with our partnerships and marketing teams to bring joint solutions to market, build relationships across our ecosystem, and help partner sellers understand where GrowthLoop fits and when to bring us into a customer conversation. This is a hands-on role: you’ll shape the plan and drive the details that deliver results.

#7316. How To Find The Questions Your Customers Ask AI (And Your Keyword Tools Miss)

Identify practical ways to improve location visibility in AI-generated answers and recommendations.

This rundown shows how to test briefs, audits, or rank reporting with a four-layer system built on your own data.

Invalid traffic on AI Max search doubled to 5.28%. Get the channel-by-channel retail benchmarks before Black Friday.

Learn how to prioritize page fixes, check agent access and measure readiness for your 2027 SEO plan.

Ask the SEJ experts you already read what AI, agents, and algorithm changes mean for your traffic. Private, human to human. $97/mo.

Webinar: 9 AI Workflows For Better Content. Nov 18, 2 PM ET.

Turn sales calls, support tickets, and forum threads into a content plan that answers the questions customers ask AI and fills gaps keyword tools miss.

In my last article, I made the case that search volume is screening out your best content opportunities. The short version is that the highest-intent questions your buyers ask are increasingly being said to an AI assistant, and they rarely show up in a keyword tool. Near the end of that piece, I listed a few places to find those questions instead: People Also Ask, forums, your own sales calls and support tickets, and the assistants themselves. That list was only four bullet points, and the most common reply I’ve had since is some version of “OK, but how do I actually do that?”

So, this is the practical follow-up. I’ll go through how I collect these questions, how I use Search Console to check which ones are already reaching a site, and how I turn the whole lot into something a content team can work from.

I always start here, because it’s the source most teams overlook, and it’s usually the best one. Sales calls, discovery calls, support tickets, live chat logs, and the messages people send before booking a demo are all people describing their problem in their own words. That’s very close to how they’d describe it to ChatGPT or Gemini.

Here’s a real example from a recent discovery call of mine (anonymized, with the details changed enough that you couldn’t identify the business). The prospect was a furniture retailer, and this was roughly how they opened the call:

“We moved to Shopify a couple of years ago and lost a big chunk of our rankings. Some of it came back, and then we dropped again this year. Is that still the migration, or are our own collection pages competing with each other?”

A few minutes later, they asked a second question:

“We sell exactly the same products in France and Germany, but those sites barely get any traffic. Do we need completely separate content for each country?”

Neither of those would ever appear in a keyword export. The closest you’d get is something like “shopify migration SEO” or “international SEO,” which tells you almost nothing about the actual situation. But if you look at those two questions as a content brief, they’re incredibly specific: a platform, a timeline, two possible causes, and a decision to make.

This is exactly the kind of question a buyer takes to an assistant, and it’s the kind of question your content should be able to answer.

If you record your calls (I use Fireflies, but most call recorders produce a transcript you can export), I’d take the last three months of sales and discovery calls and run them through an AI assistant with a prompt like this:

“From these call transcripts, list every question the prospect asked or implied. Rewrite each one the way they would phrase it to an AI assistant, and keep their constraints, such as company size, budget, timeline, platform, and what they’ve already tried. Group similar questions together and note how many calls each group came up in.”

The final instruction here is critical. If the same scenario comes up in five separate calls, that’s the closest thing to demand data you’ll get for a question with no search volume, and it’s a lot more persuasive in a meeting than “I think people are asking this.”

You want to be careful with a couple of things here. Strip out names, company details, and anything identifying before transcripts go into a third-party tool, and make sure your call recording consent covers this kind of use. Also, sales calls skew toward people who are already close to buying, so I’d add support tickets and pre-sale inquiries into the mix to capture earlier-stage questions too.

Your own calls only tell you about people who already found you. Forums help with everyone else.

Reddit is the obvious one, but Quora, industry Slack and Discord communities, Facebook groups, and review sites such as G2 or Trustpilot can all be just as useful depending on your market. The value usually lies in the opening post, where someone explains their constraints in full, and in the replies, where other people ask the follow-up questions an assistant would also need answered before it could give a decent response.

Sticking with the migration example, a search like site:reddit.com shopify migration rankings dropped should bring up plenty of threads where store owners describe the same problem in their own words, often with details my prospect didn’t mention, such as redirect issues or how long they waited before panicking.

I copy the most relevant threads into a document and run the same extraction prompt over them. That way, forum questions and sales questions end up in the same format, and you can group them together later.

See also: The Review Gap: Finding Client Opportunities In Competitor Feedback

Once you have a set of real questions, the next step is working out what an AI assistant would actually search for when answering them.

Google is open about the fact that AI Overviews and AI Mode may use a query fan-out technique, issuing multiple related searches to build a single response. You can’t see those searches for a real AI Mode session, but you can get a reasonable approximation through the Gemini API.

When you use Grounding with Google Search, the API response includes a field called webSearchQueries, which lists the searches the model ran to answer your prompt. Google’s own documentation describes it as useful for understanding the model’s reasoning process.

If you feed in the first migration question from earlier, you’d expect to see sub-queries covering things like how long rankings take to recover after a Shopify migration, how to check redirects after a re-platform, and how to spot keyword cannibalization between collection pages. Your own output will vary from run to run, but each sub-query is effectively a section your page would need to cover to be useful as a source.

You don’t need to be technical for this. Go Fish Digital published a walkthrough that runs it through Screaming Frog with a copy-and-paste custom JavaScript snippet and an API key from Google AI Studio. Their version sends each page’s H1, but swapping in your own list of questions is straightforward.

I want to be clear about what this output is, though. It shows you how a Gemini model expands a question, which is a fair proxy for Google’s AI features, but it isn’t a record of what AI Mode ran for an actual user. I treat it as a coverage checklist, and I wouldn’t present it to a client as anything more definitive than that.

For ChatGPT, Perplexity, and the other assistants, the manual route still works fine. Ask each one your question, note which sub-topics it decides to cover and which sources it cites, and compare that against the page that’s supposed to answer it.

See also: The 2003 Framework That Was Already Doing Query Fan-Out

Search Console is better as a validation step than a discovery tool for this, because it shows you which of these questions are already finding their way to your site.

Long conversational queries do turn up in the Performance report. Suganthan Mohanadasan has already covered four ways to surface them in a lot of detail, so I’d recommend checking that out if you haven’t already. For a quick look, I use the Custom (regex) option in the Query filter. This pattern returns queries of eight words or more:

And this one returns queries that start with a question word:

Then I compare what comes back against the questions from calls and forums. If the migration scenario shows up as a long query, that’s good confirmation that real people are bringing it to Google. If it doesn’t show up, it could be a coverage gap, or the queries may simply be too rare for Google to show you.

That last point is significant because Google anonymizes rare queries, and conversational queries are rare almost by definition. Suganthan found that 57.7% of his impressions sat in the anonymized pool over a 59-day period, so anything you find here is an undercount.

If you have access to the generative AI performance report, the pages view is worth a look too. It shows which of your URLs are already appearing in AI Overviews and AI Mode. There’s no query data in it (yet), and sites with few AI impressions may not see the report at all, but it does tell you which pages Google is already comfortable using as a source.

By now, you’ll have a messy list of questions from several sources. To get it into a shape a content team can actually use, I put everything into one sheet. Here’s how a single row might look, using the migration example:

For the priority column, I use the same proxies from my previous column: business value, evidence that the question is being asked, how big the coverage gap is, and whether you can answer it with real specifics. The frequency column feeds straight into the second one.

Once the sheet is filled in, you’ll usually notice that most of the questions cluster around a handful of situations. Those clusters are your content plan. In my experience, most of them can be handled by strengthening a page you already have, which is quicker than publishing something new and tends to perform better too.

I’m aware this is a lot less tidy than sorting a spreadsheet by volume, so I think it’s worth being up front about the limitations.

You still won’t have a volume figure next to any of these questions, which means you’re prioritizing on judgment and frequency. Your own calls are biased toward people who already know you exist, so questions from audiences you don’t reach yet will be underrepresented. The Gemini fan-out is an approximation of Google’s behavior, and Search Console only shows you part of the picture because of anonymization.

However, I don’t think any of that is a reason not to do it. It just means you should present the output as a well-evidenced shortlist, particularly to stakeholders who are used to seeing a number next to every target or goal.

If you want somewhere to start right now:

Keyword tools are still useful for understanding what people type into a search box. But a growing share of the questions that decide whether a buyer picks you are being asked in a conversation with an assistant, and the good news is that plenty of them are already sitting in your inbox and call recordings.

Itamar Blauer is a London-based marketing and growth leader, podcast host, author, and international speaker specialising in SEO and digital ...

Breaking news and insights to help you lead search strategies.

#7314. Snapchat will show more info on mutual friends

Let Social Media Today's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The app will allow users who opt in to see a list of friends they have in common with someone, directly on their profile.

Snapchat wants to get more people talking in the app, so it’s expanding its info display on mutual friends. The updated format will enable users to see expanded profile info for any friends that they have in common with connections.

As shown in these example screens, Snapchat’s mutual friends update will give users more info about friends they have in common directly on their profile display.

To be clear, Snapchat already displays a count of how many friends users have in common. The mutual friends feature builds on that by showing who those friends are.

That could get more people interacting, and given the slowdown of Snapchat’s audience growth, that could be a critical focus.

Snapchat’s user growth has been stagnating, especially in the U.S. and Europe, where its daily active user count has been flat through the third quarter. As such, Snapchat needs to capitalize on its existing audience in order to maximize opportunities and drive advertiser interest.

Maybe offering more specific connection tools could help boost audience interest.

The question then is whether Snapchat users will actually want to display their expanded information to friends of friends, or whether they’d prefer to keep their Snapchat friend circles exclusive.

Given the more private focus of the app, the latter seems more likely, but again, Snapchat needs to try new things in order to drive user activity.

Snapchat also said that both the user and their connections will have to opt-in to the feature before their expanded information is displayed to others.

Mutual friends is now available to Snapchat users in the U.S., Australia, France and Sweden on iOS and Android.

Camunda Hub is where enterprises run Camunda at scale. From Hub they manage their clusters and tenants, decide who can do what, and roll changes out across the whole organization. We’re looking for a Senior Product Builder to own the backend and architecture that let Hub manage a whole fleet of clusters. From there, you’ll shape Hub’s foundations: its event model, the service boundaries and data model other teams build on, and how Hub behaves when things fail or get upgraded. The platform is your product. Your users are platform administrators, Centres of Excellence and the builders across Camunda who depend on what you create. You’ll join a new two-person pod in our remote-first team. You’ll have real ownership as the person who makes the call, and you’ll build AI-first, from written specs through to production. Our customers run mission-critical processes on Camunda, so your work will matter to them every day.

Take over Hub's fleet propagation mechanism from the team building its first version, and make it work for access, secrets, tenants, licences and custom roles

Write short design documents for foundational changes, get them reviewed by the teams that depend on them, and turn them into tested, shipped code

Design how Hub behaves when things go wrong: offline clusters, partial failures, Hub outages, and clusters being adopted or detached

Build the path from clusters back to Hub, starting with deployment inventory, health, audit records and licence status

Design Hub's first event and webhook mechanism, so admins and agents can react to changes instead of polling for them

Join the pod's rotations and Hub on-call, and help other builders take on backend work through reviews, guidelines and contract tests

Experience building and running backend systems in production that other teams or customers depend on

Comfort taking a problem nobody owns yet and turning it into a design, a plan and shipped code

Product judgment for technical users: you talk to the people who use your APIs and make trade-offs on their behalf

Clear, short writing that lets a remote team review and decide asynchronously

Care for how a system behaves when parts of it fail, not only for the happy path

Designed and shipped a control plane or similar system that pushes desired state to many targets. Knows outbox or event-log patterns, idempotent apply, versioning, retries and drift reconciliation

Senior backend engineering in Java (API design, relational data models, multi-tenant SaaS services) and hands-on Helm and Kubernetes for self-managed installations

Software architecture and failure analysis: partial failure, blast radius, consistency trade-offs. Has debugged production incidents in a distributed system and is ready for on-call

Has shaped an API or platform as a product, and writes design docs other teams can build on. Gets agreement across teams without formal authority

Builds AI-first from written specifications and checks AI output as carefully as any other code

Kubernetes operators or GitOps tools, for example Argo CD

OIDC, SCIM and permission models, or secret managers and licence systems

Camunda 8 or another workflow or orchestration engine

#7307. Meta launches messaging app for teens

Let Social Media Today's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The company’s new Direct app is designed to expand Instagram DMs to a separate platform, and is almost exactly the same as the Direct app that the company launched in 2017.

Meta is testing a new, separate direct messaging app, which aims to expand Instagram DMs to its own platform, and could help Meta soften the impacts of expanding teen social media bans.

Called Direct, this latest app is aimed at teenagers, and offers a range of creation tools and features that align with teen usage behaviors.

As shown in these example images, Direct users draw on their chat threads, view inbox notes — a feature ported from IG — add stickers and voice effects, and more.

None of which is overly surprising, because Meta has actually launched this exact app before.

Back in 2017, Meta launched a messaging app for younger users, which was also called "Direct," and looks very similar to this new iteration.

That version of Direct was also focused on fun DM interactions, and included elements like drawing and effects in order to appeal to younger audiences.

Which obviously didn’t catch on then, so why is Meta now treading the same path, and trying, once again, to facilitate DM interaction between younger users?

Well, primarily, because Snapchat is struggling.

Snapchat’s user growth has stalled in the U.S. and Europe, with the app’s daily active user counts actually declining in both markets throughout the year.

With interest in Snapchat waning, Meta likely sees an opportunity to step in and provide an alternate platform for teen DMs.

It’s also worth noting that Meta launched an initial test of Direct in Turkey, which just this month enacted restrictions on social media usage for users under age 15.

As reported by Politico, Turkey’s social media ban for users under age 15 came into effect on Nov. 1. The law relates to all foreign-originated social media apps, but messaging apps are not included in the restrictions.

As such, Meta likely sees an opportunity to step in and scoop up any teens who may get booted from Snapchat as a result of this update.

Of course, Instagram and Facebook will also be impacted by these restrictions, and providing an alternative option for teen connection could ensure that Meta can soften any potential usage impacts. But an alternative messaging app won’t necessarily reduce the impact on its ads business, as it’s unlikely to inject ads into a DM focused app.

This is why Snapchat is likely the bigger focus, in terms of diluting its market share and increasing Meta’s own dominance.

In February, Snapchat reported that it had locked or disabled over 415,000 user accounts in Australia that the company identified as belonging to users under age 16, in line with Australia’s teen social media restrictions.

Australia’s population is around a third of Turkey’s, which points to the potential opportunity if Meta can replace Snap. Meta is also testing Direct in Thailand as well, which is also considering tougher teen social media restrictions, according to Thai PBS World.

Meta could use this as an opportunity to become the next critical platform for teen user engagement, with the scale of its other apps helping to promote its direct messaging app to users who would be impacted by these bans.

Meta also said that Direct will include a range of advanced protection measures, and will be subject to Meta’s parental supervision tools.

It’s an interesting tactic, though it will likely raise the eyebrows of local officials, who could see this as an attempt by Meta to circumvent social media restrictions. However, the logic applied to teen social media bans, in terms of content exposure, doesn’t necessarily apply to messaging apps.

And if Meta has the scale and capability to quickly scale messaging platforms faster than its competitors, it makes sense. And given that it’s already launched the exact same app once before, the broader effort here is likely worth the test.

#7304. AI Is transforming marketing. Can marketers prove it’s working?

Marketing leaders have more tools, data and greater automation capabilities than ever before, but is their investment actually driving business growth?

Research cited in Incubeta's 2027 Marketing Outlook found that 92% of marketing leaders believe their measurement is precise, while 41.6% acknowledge that measurement limitations waste budget, and 70.4% believe they deploy their budgets effectively.

These findings suggest that confidence in marketing performance may be outpacing the ability to demonstrate its actual contribution to business outcomes.

The challenge becomes more urgent as artificial intelligence transforms how consumers discover products and how brands engage with them. Marketers are investing in AI-powered capabilities while the customer journey becomes increasingly difficult to track.

In 2027, marketers will face greater pressure to demonstrate return on investment.

Website traffic, search rankings and conversion data have long served as key indicators of how consumers interact with brands. While these metrics have never captured the full customer journey, they have provided marketers with a relatively consistent way to measure performance.

Consumers increasingly use conversational interfaces to research products and compare alternatives without visiting brand websites. This creates a significant measurement challenge. A brand may influence a purchasing decision through an AI-generated recommendation without receiving a website visit or another traditional engagement signal.

Answer engine optimization (AEO) encourages brands to evaluate how their products and information appear in AI-generated answers. However, visibility alone offers limited insight into business impact.

Marketers can track how frequently a brand appears in AI responses to help them better understand its presence in emerging discovery channels, but determining whether that exposure influences purchasing behavior is considerably more complicated. Marketers will need to reconsider how they evaluate the contribution of activities that generate influence without producing easily attributable clicks.

AI agents that can research products and potentially complete transactions on a consumer's behalf could further complicate attribution. Shoppers might describe their requirements to an AI assistant and receive a recommendation based on their preferences. Consumers may never interact directly with the brand until the transaction is completed.

Marketers will need to learn how brands earn consideration when algorithms increasingly influence which products are presented to customers. The consideration of a brand may depend increasingly on the quality of its product information and its relevance to specific consumer needs. Widespread adoption of agentic commerce is not guaranteed, but it illustrates why existing measurement approaches may become less effective as consumer journeys evolve.

AI is also transforming how marketing campaigns are developed and executed. Generative tools allow brands to produce and adapt creative assets at unprecedented speed. Organizations that manage campaigns across multiple markets can see considerable efficiency gains. However, producing more content does not necessarily improve marketing performance.

Incubeta's recent white paper showed that 77% of marketing leaders express confidence in their creative assets, even though many still assess effectiveness after media budgets have already been spent.

This creates a risk that AI will accelerate spending on creative executions whose actual contribution remains uncertain. Marketers shouldn't slow down creative automation but should pair it with testing built for the same pace. This will help marketers better understand which creative elements influence purchasing behavior.

Marketers can turn automation from a volume engine into a growth engine by connecting creative production directly to testing and measurement. Using that data, marketers can adapt their strategies and then share those insights back into the next round of production.

AI changes how brands have traditionally evaluated their marketing effectiveness. Advertising platforms provide detailed performance reporting but may stop measuring activities within their own environments. Now multiple platforms can claim credit for the same conversations, but investments that create demand over a longer period might receive insufficient recognition.

These limitations become especially consequential when marketing leaders use channel-level metrics to decide overall spending.

A more comprehensive approach combines marketing mix modeling (MMM) with incrementality testing and business performance data.

MMM can estimate the contribution of different marketing investments over time, while incrementality experiments help determine whether specific activities generated additional outcomes.

Combining these approaches allows marketers to evaluate spending decisions against broader commercial objectives, including profitability and customer value. The goal is to build a more reliable basis for deciding where to increase investment, where to decrease it and which activities contribute to sustainable growth.

AI's growing influence is also changing expectations for marketing leadership.

CMOs are being asked to evaluate emerging technologies while demonstrating financial accountability to CEOs and CFOs. This requires closer coordination between marketing and finance, particularly when assessing investments whose benefits may develop over time.

It also demands greater discipline around experimentation.

New capabilities should be evaluated against clearly defined business questions, with appropriate measures of success established before significant investment.

The organizations best positioned for 2027 will be those that can connect technological adoption with evidence of improved performance.

AI will continue to create opportunities for marketers to operate more efficiently and engage consumers in new ways. But greater technological sophistication will mean little if organizations cannot establish whether those investments are contributing to growth.

The defining challenge for marketing leaders will be closing the gap between what their technology can do and what they can prove it delivers.

Major marketers looking to engage U.S. consumers around the milestone must prove their bona fides or risk looking like bandwagoners.

#7303. Snapchat shares insight into luxury shopping trends

Let Social Media Today's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

A new study commissioned by the app found that 84% of its users said social media platforms made them feel close to the luxury universe.

Snapchat has published a new report which examines key trends among luxury shoppers, as well as the role that Snapchat itself plays in connecting shoppers to new luxury products.

The report is based on a survey of more than 9,000 luxury consumers across nine global markets, which was conducted by APP Media and Ifop group.

The data underlines the role that social media plays in raising awareness of luxury products, and specifically highlights Snapchat’s role in helping to inform and validate luxury shopping decisions.

The data shows that 76% of luxury shoppers agreed that social platforms make them feel close to the luxury universe. Snapchat users are even more aligned with this, with 84% of them agreeing with the statement.

The report also looked at how luxury shoppers approach buying, including their motivations for shopping for higher-end products.

The report also includes a range of helpful data points for marketing plans, such as info on the impact of scarcity.

In addition, the report also explored the role that peer validation plays in the luxury goods purchase process.

The latter is an important note for Snapchat specifically, because users in the app generally engaged with smaller groups of close friends within their message streams.

Indeed, the survey data showed that content from close connections is the key driver of luxury purchase decisions for Snapchatters.

The report also included notes on the kinds of content luxury shoppers respond to, which could help to guide strategic marketing approaches.

These are some interesting and potentially valuable notes for luxury brands, offering specific insights into how social platforms, and Snapchat specifically, can help to amplify messaging and drive purchase intent.

#7301. How CeraVe’s ‘Dune’ deal takes the brand’s social-first playbook global

The L’Oréal brand’s first film partnership makes it the “Official Moisturizer of Arrakis” ahead of the blockbuster’s Dec. 18 release.

CeraVe last week partnered with “Dune: Part Three” to become the “Official Moisturizer of Arrakis,” the desert planet that serves as the sci-fi epic’s titular setting. The tie-up with Warner Bros. Pictures and Legendary Pictures represents the L'Oréal brand’s first film partnership, with a campaign that will roll out ahead of the movie’s Dec. 18 theatrical release.

The brand on Monday revealed the next chapter in the campaign. A humorous “Sandworm Wrangler” video, shot in the style of behind-the-scenes content, shows how a wrangler of one of the film’s iconic sandworms uses CeraVe Moisturizing Cream — which now offers 120 hours of hydration — on the dry-skinned creature.

The debut “Precious Resource” spot reimagined the lore of Arrakis, an arid planet where every drop of moisture is preserved, replacing the story’s central resource, spice, with CeraVe. The “Dune” partnership is the latest iteration of a “medutainment” strategy that combines science with entertainment and has helped grow CeraVe into a $2 billion brand.

“When it comes to the playbook, that's something we are reproducing here with ‘Dune,’” said Vincent Chauvière, global brand president at CeraVe. “It's a multipoint campaign that is social-first, starting with a bit of excitement and surprise, and then we are going full media.”

Along with co-branded content, the campaign spans paid, earned, social, digital, retail, creator and experiential activations. The brand will also launch out-of-home ads, creator gifting and corporate screening experiences, as well as sponsor film premiere events in Los Angeles and the U.K.

“Our goal is to create partnerships that extend the worlds of our films in ways that feel unexpected, culturally relevant and genuinely entertaining,” Julie Moore, executive vice president, global brand partnerships at Warner Bros. Pictures, said in a statement.

The “Dune” campaign was created and produced by OBB Bolded, OBB’s branded content studio, with content filmed in Samalayuca Dunes in Mexico and Mexico City and directed by Maca Rubio from Stink Films. OBB was also behind an award-winning “Moisturize Like a Derm” effort that appointed NBA star Kevin Durant as the brand’s “Face of Legs.”

“It's been a campaign that has been working at bringing awareness to the brand, but also to the usage of a body moisturizer, bringing our science to new consumers, particularly men. We've been recruiting more men this year, thanks to this partnership [with the NBA],” Chauvière explained.

The “Dune” partnership is rolling out globally, and the campaign is designed to pair content around the billion-dollar franchise with market-specific dermatologists and experiential activations. CeraVe is growing at a double-digit rate this year and gaining share around the world, and geographic expansion is key to the brand’s future growth, Chauvière said.

“The partnership with ‘Dune’ [is] a way to have a more consistent brand tone of voice and point of view. We have local teams that have a lot of local activations, and the influencer world is a local world. So it's all all about the balance,” the executive said.

The local push builds on CeraVe’s larger creator strategy, which helps the brand reach more than 15 million followers across platforms. The brand this summer united over 100 creators from 22 countries for its first Global CerAwards in Los Angeles. The event included CeraVe Studios, an immersive content creation experience hosted on a Hollywood studio lot, that generated nearly 60 million views of new content, per Chauvière.

“It was a moment of visibility, but it's also a moment to gather all the local influencers together and reunite around this idea of bringing creativity in the beauty world and making science accessible and entertaining at the same time,” the executive said.

In a world where many social apps measure their success by time spent online, Places offers a different kind of product built by Raya, a technology company focused on providing utility. Our apps, Raya and Places, are tools for discovery of and access to exciting people and places globally. We believe that by marrying great software with a membership-based community built around core values of trust, curation, and authenticity, we can provide solutions that offer something different to infinite content and AI-slop. Places’ mission is to help discerning global travelers find their favorite places wherever they go through expert curation and community trust.

As a Data Scientist on the Places by Raya team you will play a key role that will shape how the Places app uses data to drive business decisions. This hands-on role combines data modeling and reporting with product analysis and experimentation. While the product itself has existed since 2023 the culture is very much like a new start-up with leadership being heavily invested in this product space and this role being the first dedicated Data Scientist to this space and supported by a dedicated Data Engineer.

We are seeking an entrepreneurial and driven data scientist to identify opportunities to improve retention and help the team evaluate and act on them. This will be a fun role for someone who wants to build data solutions from the ground up and help shape how the entire team uses insights. This person should be able to articulate best practices, develop new analytical frameworks that tie user actions to outcomes, and strike the right balance between analytical rigor and pragmatic business action.

Successful candidates will demonstrate technical skills, product expertise, and business acumen, and be enthusiastic about making a positive impact through timely execution. You are passionate about leveraging the power of data to drive product changes with quality and agility and can demonstrate sound judgment under uncertainty.

#7296. Deloitte Digital takes the holdco operating system pitch straight to the CEO

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

CEOs have never cared much for holdcos. Enterprise solutions, on the other hand, get a meeting. Which makes Deloitte Digital’s new operating system for marketers an awkward one for the rest of the industry.

It’s sold as a license, though not the kind a CMO can approve alone. Designed eventually to stretch across sales, marketing, service and commerce, it’s the kind of deal Deloitte Digital’s U.S. CMO Mark Singer called a “more executive conversation.” That conversation happens in the C-suite, well above the marketing department where holdcos usually pitch.

The product is called Converge Front Office. It strings together AI agents, some built by Deloitte and others a client already runs elsewhere, to handle marketing and sales work from research and briefs through to campaign deployment and deal tracking. Two parts are live, a marketing operating system and a sales one, with a commerce version on the way. Singer pitched them as pieces of one front office system built around an idea the consulting firm calls “brand to cash” — every step of marketing and sales, from building a brand to closing a deal, should be traceable back to revenue.

“It’s not just about the agents, it’s about the task orchestration among them,” said Singer.

If that sounds familiar, it’s because holdcos have spent the better part of two years saying much the same. It carries more weight from a consulting firm, though, and Singer made a point of setting Deloitte’s version apart. The market is full of AI tools, he said, and “everybody’s doing a press release every 20 minutes on them.” Some exist to make an agency more efficient for its cleints. Others sell smarter work off the back of an owner’s media insights. Deloitte wants to build tools “that we hand off to our clients,” said Singer. From there, they can run them in-house or use them as a way of “rethinking their agency infrastructure,” he added.

A demo showed how that could play out. Umberto Castaldo, Deloitte Digital’s Converge marketing go-to-market lead, ran a campaign for a dummy sneaker brand. Agents passed the work along from market research and media modelling to the brief, the content, quality assurance and finally deployment into the brand’s existing mar tech. All those agents are routed into the consulting firm’s wider tech stack. Some of them draw on Deloitte’s own asset along the way. The research agent, for instance, can tap Deloitte’s own third-party data spine alongside client data.

Of those steps, content is doing a fair share of the selling, at least in the system’s current form. In the consulting firm’s view, brands have already paid for far more content than they’ve used.

Big brands have spent hundreds of millions, if not billions, on content over the past five years, Castaldo argued, and little of it has run recently. The system’s answer is to put that back catalog to work. Tagging agents can first relabel what sits in a brand’s asset library so other agents can find it. In the demo, those agents then built email and display ads for three audiences out of images and headlines the sneaker brand already had on file. Each asset is tracked from there, so the brand can see how it performs from one campaign to the next.

The sales side runs in parallel. Abe Awasthi, Deloitte Digital’s AI sales transformation leader, showed how its built around a seller’s day: the gap to quote, which deals need attention and where new demand might come from. That last job falls to an agent called Scout, which reads campaign results from the marketing side and matches them to a rep’s target customers. Administrators decide how far each agent can go, from suggesting to drafting to acting.

“Autonomy should be earned,” said Awasthi.

For now, most early clients are using the two halves separately, though Singer said some are starting to take both. Joint marketing and sales deals remain early, with one or two pitches in the past year, Singer said. As clients mature, he expects them to see the value in the “white space in between.”

The consulting firm shared one result. A client gave half of its 300 business development reps the sales operating system. Over a three-month pilot, the response rate on their outbound emails went from 17% to around 56%. Once the rollout scaled, that settled in the 30s — roughly double the starting point. Quotas for the team up 20%, said Awasthi, and the client is hiring more reps. Marketing teams are similarly seeing early gains in throughput, said Castaldo, who didn’t share any specifics.

Like everything the consulting firm has tried in advertising, Converge is designed to sit above it all, and Singer was blunt about where that leads. He expects “everything will be disrupted” across marketing services and said the shift is already underway. Four large clients he wouldn’t name are pulling their marketing into global capability centers, which he said is now possible without losing the personalization each market and language needs. Deloitte Digital already runs centralized functions like these for clients. They cover most of the marketing work, though brand advertising sits outside them. Setting up a function on that scale. Singer added, means building what he called “captive environments” for it.

In outsourcing, a “captive” is an operation a company owns and runs for itself. The brand keeps the people and the budget. The system they all work inside is another matter, and that’s what the largest marketing services groups are now chasing. Consulting firms like Deloitte have the advantage of already being in the room where that gets decided. The holdcos have been trying to get there for a while. At CES in January, Omnicom, Stagwell, Havas and WPP each unveiled agentic platforms built to sit on top of a client’s existing tools and run the work between them. WPP has since gone further, recasting itself as a single company with four divisions: WPP Media, WPP Production, WPP Enterprise Solutions and the newly-created WPP Creative. Publicis started earlier, paying $3.5 billion for Sapient in 2015 and $4.4 billion for Epsilon in 2019. Software companies are doing the same. Adobe used its conference earlier this month to introduce its end-to-end content supply chain product.

“One of the secret weapons of the management consultancy agencies in gaining market share during the past 10 years has been leveraging their established relationships with client CEOs, CIOs, and Boards of Directors,” said Gartner vp analyst Jay Wilson. “These relationships, and the credibility and sway those agencies have with those executives often allowed them to intercept opportunities before an agency review took place. Increasingly, we see and hear from clients and agencies that the CFO is the critical piece of the new business puzzle, as marketing budgets remain flat, and CMOs are pressured to prove the value of marketing, including agency investments.”

Marketers use creator, influencer and tastemaker interchangeably, but the industry still hasn’t settled on what the labels mean.

A new product manager role will explore ad-related monetization, suggesting the platform could make ads a bigger part of its business.

Marketers expect their 2026 holiday revenue to increase 1% to 31% or more in comparison to their 2025 holiday revenue.

#7293. Software Engineer - AI Tooling Infrastructure at Supabase

Supabase is the Postgres development platform, built by developers for developers. We provide a complete backend solution including Database, Auth, Storage, Edge Functions, Realtime, and Vector Search. All services are deeply integrated and designed for growth.

We’re looking for a Software Engineer, AI Tooling Infrastructure to join our AI Tooling team and help build and run the backend services behind Supabase’s AI products. The Supabase MCP server is one example of what the team runs in production, alongside the services that support agent skills and the dashboard assistant. You’ll work closely with the Platform, SRE, Auth, Billing and Edge Functions teams, and with the engineers on the AI Tooling team who build the product surfaces.

The AI Tooling team runs its own services in production and carries its own on-call. Traffic from AI clients and agents behaves differently from traffic from people: hundreds of calls a minute, retry loops, and no reading of deprecation warnings. This role makes sure the services they use are reliable, correctly authorized and observable. It is a systems and infrastructure role rather than a product AI role, and it is not an SRE role. You will build and operate these systems, not only advise on them.

This role is ideal for someone who thrives in async, fast-paced environments, owns what they ship in production, and is excited about building developer tools that scale to millions.

Build and operate the production backend services behind Supabase’s AI products, including the MCP server: deployment, scaling, configuration and release.

Take ownership of how those services are structured, including splitting or reshaping a service when that makes it safer to run and easier to change.

Define, measure and meet reliability targets for the services the team runs. Set SLOs with the SRE team, track error budgets, and use them to decide what to fix first.

Participate in the AI Tooling team on-call rotation, and be a first responder for incidents in those services. Investigate, fix, coordinate with other teams during incidents, and write the follow-ups and runbooks.

Work the AI Tooling team support queue alongside on-call, and turn recurring issues into fixes rather than repeated manual answers.

Own authentication and authorization for AI and agent clients: OAuth apps, scoped tokens, and permissions that make sense for agents as well as humans, including how we change or deprecate a permission without breaking existing integrations.

Own observability for the services the team runs: metrics, traces, logs, dashboards and alerts, so we find problems before customers do.

Handle capacity and abuse for agent traffic: rate limits, cost and safety controls, and behavior under load or when a dependency degrades.

Improve the architecture of the services the team owns as they grow, including splitting or reshaping a service when that makes it safer to run and easier to change.

Work with the Platform team on shared code, review ownership and release process, and with the SRE team on infrastructure, alerting and incident practice.

Contribute to the code of the AI Tooling team’s tools, such as the MCP server, where it affects reliability and security, in partnership with the engineers who own the product surface.

Have 5+ years of experience building and running backend services in production, and have carried on-call for them.

Write strong TypeScript or Go, and are comfortable working in the other.

Have designed and operated APIs with real consumers: versioning, error handling, rate limiting, and backwards-compatible change.

Know OAuth, OIDC, JWT and API authorization well enough to debug them in production and to explain where implementations commonly deviate from the spec.

Have a good understanding of PostgreSQL and relational databases.

Are comfortable with CI/CD, Docker and containerized deployments, and with infrastructure as code. Any tool is fine.

Can debug across services: follow a slow or failing request through several systems using metrics, traces and logs.

Communicate clearly in writing, work with a wide range of stakeholders, and thrive in async, globally distributed teams.

Are comfortable navigating ambiguity and moving quickly.

Pick your centre of gravity. We expect you to be deep in at least one of these, and to tell us which:

APIs and auth: You have designed permission models, token scopes or OAuth flows for third-party or machine clients, and you have migrated them without breaking users.

Operations: You have run production services yourself: carried the pager, written alerts and runbooks, used SLOs or similar to decide what to fix, and made shared systems less risky over time.

Gateways and distributed systems: You have built or run proxies or gateways, or debugged hard failures across services (retries, partial failures, upstream outages, fail-open vs fail-closed decisions), and you can reason about load and degradation.

Experience with agent tooling (MCP or similar), or serving traffic from AI agents and LLM clients.

Experience moving a service out of a larger codebase while keeping existing clients working.

Experience with Grafana, OpenTelemetry, Logflare or a similar observability stack, and real SLO work.

Open-source contributions, especially to Node.js, Go, TypeScript or API tooling.

We hire globally. We believe you can do your best work from anywhere. There are no Supabase offices, but we provide a WeWork membership or co-working allowance you can use anywhere in the world.

Every team member receives ESOP (equity ownership) in the company. We want everyone to share in the upside of what we’re building together.

Use this budget to set up your ideal work environment—laptop, monitor, headphones, or whatever helps you do your best work.

Supabase covers 100% of health insurance for employees and 80% for dependents, wherever you are. Your wellbeing and your family’s health are important to us.

Once a year, the entire company gathers in a new city for a week of connection, collaboration, and fun. It’s a highlight of our year.

We operate asynchronously and trust you to manage your own time. You know what needs to be done and when.

Every team member receives an annual education allowance to spend on learning—courses, books, conferences, or anything that supports your growth.

Supabase was born-remote and open-source-first. We believe our globally distributed team is our secret weapon in building tools developers love.

#7289. How Bustle Digital Group is helping Pampers and Innisfree show up in AI recommendations

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

Bustle Digital Group is using Profound’s AI measurement tools to help brands, including Pampers and Innisfree, show up in AI-generated recommendations.

The publisher’s GEO offering, called ECHO internally, uses Profound analytics and AI agents trained on content from BDG’s titles to generate creative briefs and guidelines. BDG’s creative team uses those materials to produce content designed to be cited by AI platforms. At least three brands have paid for the offering.

An uptick in client inquiries this year prompted BDG to develop the offering, said Graham Howes, svp of distribution and AI intelligence at BDG, which owns titles like Bustle, Nylon, Elite Daily and Scary Mommy.

BDG is the only publisher currently working with Profound to turn this process into an advertiser offering.

In one campaign, which ran between Aug 11–Sep 30, Korean beauty brand Innisfree’s visibility across 200 tracked skincare prompts grew 30%, while Bustle’s citation rank rose from #709 to #12, according to a BDG case study based on Profound data.

“By combining trusted editorial environments, answer engine optimization, and prompt-level measurement, we were able to demonstrate that AI visibility can be measured, optimized, and ultimately transformed into a meaningful competitive advantage,” said Julien Bouzitat, gm of Innisfree U.S., part of the Amore Pacific Group.

Baby care brand Pampers moved from #2 to #1 in AI visibility for fragrance-free diaper questions following a Scary Mommy explainer addressing Swaddlers’ fragrance-free switch. The explainer ran between Sep 4–17.

“There isn’t a defined playbook for how publishers should be approaching this. So we really had to come at this from a very DIY place of first understanding the space,” Howes said.

The process begins with an RFP and conversations with the brand on their AI visibility goals, Howes explained. Then comes a two- to four-week baseline research phase, where BDG and Profound analyze eight to 10 topics that the advertiser wants to increase its visibility against within an AI answer engine, and 25 to 50 prompts [on each topic] to understand the brand’s competitive standing and what sites the answer engines are currently citing.

BDG then uses its own AI agents, trained on its titles’ content, to develop an editorial strategy for the branded content. This includes potential article topics, headline structure, and tone, Howes explained. That strategy is then presented to the advertiser.

“The advertiser has no hand in the actual creative development at all. We leave that entirely up to our editors, partly because we want to make sure that we are following all of the best practices of what gets cited, and something that is very heavy-handed on the advertorial front is very unlikely to do that,” Howes said.

AI agents within Profound then design the creative briefs, which get handed off to BDG’s creative team. They write in their own voice, but follow guidelines set by the agents, such as including an FAQ section at the bottom of the article, or the type of article format, such as a listicle or an explainer.

“You’ve got to be able to connect analytics into action,” said Josh Blyskal, head of insights at Profound.

Once the content is live, it’s cross-promoted on Reddit, where BDG is running paid media. This helps build awareness among relevant communities of Reddit users, which “still matters a lot in the overall algorithm of what is being picked up and why,” Howes said. Reddit, he added, remains a significant citation source for answer engines.”

BDG tracks the performance of those posts throughout the campaign, he added.

This is the big question, especially given attribution is difficult because AI platforms offer limited insight into how people use their answers to make purchasing decisions, making it hard for publishers to attribute AI’s influence on traffic, engagement and conversions.

BDG tracks the performance of the GEO-branded content by analyzing metrics including the brand’s visibility on AI platforms, and BDG’s citation share and citation rank, mainly across ChatGPT, Gemini and Google’s AI Overviews.

Profound measures AI visibility by whether the brand appears in an AI response. Citation share measures the percentage of total citations in a given set of AI-generated answers that come from a particular publisher’s site, Blyskal said. (Citation share is typically between 0.1% and 3%, he noted.) Citation rank measures how a publisher’s content ranks against competitors based on how frequently each is cited in AI-generated answers.

“We want to really be able to prove to the best of our ability, that these two goals are interconnected when we run programs in this kind of way,” Howes said.

Using Profound, BDG identifies examples of its content appearing in AI answers, monitors which articles generate the most citations and makes real-time optimizations to improve performance, Howes said. Advertisers then receive a report detailing the results.

“Now the question is, does the influence layer – i.e., ChatGPT, where people find the conviction to decide what to buy – net cleanly into the transaction layer, which is often through Google into the website? And the answer is no, not right now,” Blyskal said.

Howes declined to share how much revenue its GEO offering was generating, or how much BDG was charging brands for this offering. It’s sold as a standalone offering, or bundled into existing custom content packages. Profound does not receive a cut of these deals.

“We see this more as an extension of [our] custom content opportunities, not a replacement to them,” Howes said. “Clients can still have those opportunities to reach our audiences on our sites, on our social channels, but also pair that with these types of opportunities to also reach similar audiences that are now choosing to engage an answer engine to have a question answered.”

Ashish Jacob, director of GEO at Go Fish Digital, said consistent measurement matters more than which analytics platforms a company uses to track visibility.

“The most valuable metrics include how often a brand appears in relevant AI responses, which sources are cited, sentiment, competitive share of voice, and how those results change over time. It’s also important to understand the prompts, AI platforms, and sampling methods behind the data, since different tools may produce different results. First-party reporting is becoming increasingly valuable as well,” Jacob said.

Profound is seeing some renewals for 2027, as well as exploring running always-on, yearlong campaigns, Howes said.

“A lot of advertisers realize that this content is very powerful, but it also has a half-life to it, and if you want to really make sure that you have an ongoing presence – especially when we think about our partners in beauty and fashion, where so much of what they’re doing is very seasonal – that they need to really have a more always-on approach to their content strategy,” Howes said.

These GEO offerings require publishers to be nimble, Howes said. Because AI answer engines are constantly changing their algorithms, GEO requires ongoing monitoring, research and content optimization, he said. Publishers must continually adapt their strategies to keep brands visible in AI-generated answers. But that’s an opportunity in itself, to act “not just as content partners” but as “a knowledge resource” to help brands understand what’s happening within AI answer engine recommendations, he added.

Howes sees GEO as an opportunity not just to improve its own visibility in AI search, but to grow its advertising business. Branded and custom content is BDG’s largest revenue source, he said. Measuring and demonstrating how GEO efforts benefit advertisers will be critical to strengthening brand partnerships and expanding these offerings as a revenue stream, Howes noted.

“I really want to better understand how this new paradigm we find ourselves in really plays a direct role in our business, not just our own editorial growth. To be able to have that kind of data and visibility, not just in our own efforts, but in our advertisers’ efforts as well, is fundamental to what we do because we need to be able to have that level of transparency to grow the kind of partnerships that we’re looking to grow into next year with these types of packages,” Howes said.

Creators are tapping into their massive content libraries for syndication opportunities that pay dividends.

More brands are gravitating toward live shopping, a growing channel that is set to play an outsized influence on shopping trends.

Twitch is swinging big with a new short-form content push that should help more creators get discovered on-platform.

#7286. Amazon puts Arnold Schwarzenegger in Santa suit to extend holiday push

A new spot ties into the upcoming movie “The Man with the Bag,” showing Amazon again tapping into its own programming to promote its range of offerings.

Amazon is once again tapping into its Prime ecosystem to integrate one of its entertainment properties into an advertising campaign. Taking inspiration from “The Man with the Bag,” which premiers Dec. 2, the e-commerce platform has created a light-hearted holiday ad featuring a familiar face.

“Santa Schwarzenegger” follows the actor as he uses a Santa suit, delivered by Prime, to explore New York City unnoticed. During his adventure, Schwarzenegger is able to watch a game with bar patrons, catch a holiday movie and use his strength to help a family get a Christmas tree on the roof of their car. It ends with the actor using an Amazon Locker to pick up a pair of skates so he can enjoy time on an ice rink. The minute-long spot is set to “Make Someone Happy” by Jimmy Durante.

Schwarzenegger will also appear as Santa throughout the season outside of the spot and movie, per press details.

Amazon has made finding moments of joy a central part of its holiday strategy. “Santa Schwarzenegger” follows the longer “Joy Delivered” spot, which tells the story of a young woman going on a “Planes, Trains and Automobiles”-style journey to make it home to her family in time for the holidays, giving away the gifts she bought along the way.

This is not the first time Amazon has drawn inspiration from a Prime Video title in its advertising. In May, Amazon’s Brand Innovation Lab released a spot with Liquid I.V. featuring the leading actors from its hockey romance show, “Off Campus,” marking the first time Prime Video leads appeared in a co-branded spot.

“Santa Schwarzenegger” allows the e-commerce giant to promote its delivery services while also showing how its properties can be used for advertising.

Amazon’s ad revenue hit $19.6 billion in Q3, a 12% year-over-year increase, per an earnings statement. However, this is a significant dip from the 26% YoY growth rate seen just a quarter earlier, leading to a stock dip for the company.
