#7241. Why Qualcomm teamed with F1’s new star in race for sports marketing ROI

The company’s consumer brand Snapdragon signed 20-year-old phenom Kimi Antonelli as a brand ambassador as it looks to connect with racing’s growing audience.

Snapdragon, the consumer brand of chipmaker Qualcomm, on Tuesday launched an anthem film around its partnership with Formula 1 driver Kimi Antonelli. The 20-year-old driver for the Mercedes-AMG Petronas team — of which the brand is also a partner — became the youngest Formula 1 World Champion ever earlier this month.

The video is part of a campaign timed to the 2026 Las Vegas Grand Prix on Nov. 21. It will run on F1 broadcasts outside the U.S. on Sky, on digital and in partnership with channels including Sports Illustrated and YouTube. Apple TV is the exclusive broadcast partner of F1 in the U.S., which prevents brands like Snapdragon from running traditional broadcast ads around the race.

“We've got other ways to bring it to life,” Qualcomm CMO Don McGuire said of the campaign. “We've got creators involved, we've got remixing involved, we have slipstreaming involved… hopefully turning it into an anthem for him that has really long legs.”

A 60-second hero video follows Antonelli during a day at a hotel. Wearing smart glasses and using a tablet, both powered by Snapdragon chips, the phenom listens to a version of the traditional Neapolitan song “Funiculì, Funiculà,” trains in the gym and kicks off a social media trend. The ad ends with a tagline, “Jamme, Jamme. To the top!,” which quotes the song and speaks to his meteoric rise in racing.

“We've been watching Kimi for quite some time, and we have always believed that he was meant for greatness and that he was going to ascend very quickly. I think we made the right bet,” McGuire said.

Snapdragon’s partnership with Antonelli builds on the brand’s deal with Mercedes-AMG Petronas, a strategic collaboration that kicked off in 2023. Qualcomm had previously partnered with the team in the 2010s, a deal that was not “ROI-driven, marketing-driven,” said McGuire, who became CMO in 2021. The new partnership looks to better connect the brand’s objectives with the growing audience of F1, both in the U.S. and globally.

“The sport more and more is not something that I can judge based on [advertising value equivalency]. We have some pretty good placements on the Mercedes cars, especially with cockpit cameras and things like that, but it's just so fleeting, from a visibility standpoint,” McGuire said.

Those issues are exacerbated by the Apple TV deal, which limits brands’ ability to measure impact during broadcasts. Qualcomm is committed to F1, Mercedes-AMG Petronas and Antonelli, especially as it seeks to bring the partnership from content and storytelling through activations and hospitality.

“We believe we're already seeing higher ROI coming out of some things that we've done in the short term,” McGuire said. “We believe in the sport, why we were there and the fundamentals.”

Outside of F1, Snapdragon is no stranger to sports. Since the start of the 2024-25 season, the brand has been the principal shirt sponsor of Manchester United. Despite the iconic soccer team’s on- and off-field issues in recent years, the deal has yielded “humongous ROI” for Snapdragon, McGuire said.

“When you're dealing with a sports brand that big, at scale, the brand can withstand the ebbs and flows of performance on the pitch because the fan base is so huge,” the executive explained. “That's the benchmark that we use [for sports partnerships] and maybe it's a little unfair because they are arguably the biggest sports brand in the world.”

Snapdragon’s latest F1 marketing comes on the heels of a holiday campaign that launched earlier this month. At the heart of “The Gift That Changes Everything” is a sweet 60-second hero spot that shows how a mom and dad make Christmas seem to come sooner. The parents use a Snapdragon-powered PC to learn how to build a "time machine" that gives their child a special Christmas morning experience.

The campaign is running across broadcast, connected TV and paid social in the U.S. with CTV and paid social in the U.K., Germany and France. Created in partnership with 72andSunny New York and 72andSunny Toronto, the effort iterates on a “Snapdragon. That’s How” creative platform that launched earlier this year. The campaign has seen strong preliminary results, McGuire reported.

The holiday creative arrives as the brand prepares for a season that could see hardware makers impacted by memory shortages, reducing Snapdragon’s total addressable market at a time when the Nvidia scandal is roiling the chip market. The brand continues to work to build awareness and affinity, regardless of short-term market conditions.

“This is about building muscle and emotion that can sustain the long-term,” McGuire said. “We feel really strongly that we had to lean into holiday as if it were any other holiday season, regardless of the macro conditions.”

#7237. WTF is a unified media platform?

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

This article is a WTF explainer, in which we break down media and marketing’s most confusing terms. More from the series →

Editor’s note: These technologies are ill-defined, please share feedback to “editorial@digiday.com” to talk more.

Ad tech has never been short on acronyms. Its latest crop includes: unified advertising platforms (UAPs), unified media platforms (UMPs), and unified buying platforms (UBPs).

This kind of language has added a new layer of confusion to the space, and ad executives across adland have been saying, “WTF…?” Digiday is here to help by trying to bring some clarity to the matter.

A simple way to approach the question is to think of such terms — UAP, UBP and UMP — as overlapping labels for technology that promises to make advertising easier to buy, manage and optimize across an increasingly fragmented media landscape.

Of course, AI is central to the pitch. Advocates of such technologies maintain that instead of separately operating campaigns across Google, Meta, Amazon and the open web, advertisers could potentially use one system to determine where money goes, then execute those decisions through the relevant platforms.

Some providers propose bringing buying and selling infrastructure together. Others want to sit above existing systems and coordinate them. This is an important distinction to keep in mind, as connective software is not the same as controlling how an advertiser spends its next dollar.

At its simplest, a UMP coordinates planning, buying, optimization, and measurement across multiple media channels. Its remit could include programmatic display, CTV, retail media, search, social, and direct publisher relationships.

According to most sources, the idea is not necessarily to replace every existing media trading infrastructure, i.e., demand-side and supply-side platforms, etc., but to make them work together more efficiently.

That is distinct from the traditional remit of a DSP, many of which have spent years expanding the inventory and formats available through their own buying interfaces. A unified platform, by contrast, promises to operate across buying environments that no single DSP controls.

Jay Friedman, co-founder of CartographAI, told Digiday that the emerging proposition is about budget fluidity, not simply consolidating DSPs and SSPs. “We define a UMP as a platform that executes buys across all primary digital channels (search, social, programmatic, and direct). This may or may not include agents,” Friedman told Digiday. “Traditionally, this has been the domain of agency order management systems,” he said, noting how several new entrants are hoping to challenge these players.

The terms overlap, but they differ in what they promise to unify. The chart below traces that range, from handling an individual transaction to coordinating campaigns across channels and deciding where budgets go.

Keith Weisberg of Unplugged Collective told Digiday UBPs would initially come to market as a repackaging of existing tools. He said the deeper shift would come when buyers could bring their data to the places ads run and delegate spending decisions to AI agents.

“I’d treat that as a net-new product, not a DSP feature,” he said. “However, we’re all plugged into DSPs, and even if it’s a new product, it’ll roll out via our current commercial relationships, because that trust (and legal contracting) took years.”

Ana Milicevic, co-founder of Sparrow Advisers, described the labels as directional distinctions rather than settled product categories. She added, “It’s not all just a collection of black boxes. Then that’s a net new layer in the stack.” The key distinction is whether a platform merely pushes buttons in existing interfaces or makes independent decisions about where budgets go.

Ciarán O’Kane, general partner at First Party Capital, was more skeptical about novelty. In wording expressly cleared for attribution, he characterized UMPs and UBPs as “meta DSPs” that act as workflow layers on top of existing systems by plugging into APIs. He also said many emerging players in the space that use this terminology are revisiting approaches the industry has tried before.

Media-buying teams have long struggled with the fragmentation of conducting ad campaigns using different buying interfaces, datasets, and measurement methods. AI can make the operational work of combining and interpreting those inputs less expensive, but it does not automatically create comparable performance data or independent buying authority.

“The frustration is very, very old. What’s new now is that AI is letting us operationally skip through some hurdles that were previously insurmountable,” Milicevic told Digiday. Tasks that once demanded a substantial analytics stack and specialist labor can increasingly be assisted by repeatable prompts and automated processes.

The commercial question is whether cheaper orchestration creates an entirely new buyer or simply a more convenient way to operate existing platforms. For advertisers, that distinction determines whether the new technology saves money, reallocates money, or merely adds another vendor to pay.

In an Oct. 8 blog post, titled “universal buying platform,” Apostrophe co-founders Brian O’Kelley and Anne Coghlan outlined a broader buying layer intended to connect intent, execution, and measurement across advertising environments, citing the recent launch of Amazon Ads Agent as an example of this trend.

Their argument is a public proposal, not evidence that a single independent platform already performs every part of the workflow at scale — O’Kelley did not respond to Digiday’s request for comment by press time.

Other companies are approaching the problem from different directions, with Olyzon — an entity that used the term “agentic orchestration platform” in its recent CTV launch material — serving as one example.

Jules Minvielle, co-founder and CEO of Olyzon, told Digiday the company does not own inventory and uses agents to coordinate buying through existing platforms, with advertiser-controlled approvals. “A DSP is one place where you can buy. Orchestration is all the work around the buy: building the plan, deciding where each dollar goes, activating across every platform, watching delivery, measuring, and feeding what worked into the next plan,” he wrote in an emailed statement, adding that uneven API access and inconsistent measurement remain substantial obstacles.

Ravi Patel, co-founder and CEO of SWYM.ai, drew a distinction between a common interface and control over the budget. “It can manage the ‘what’ and the ‘how much.’ It can’t manage the ‘which impression.’ That distinction is the whole story,” he explained, adding that campaign setup and pacing across platforms are already possible through APIs. However, impression-level optimization within the major walled gardens remains in their own systems.

Lucas Cridland, CEO of Basin Media, questioned whether agents are changing the underlying buying process. “At the moment, many environments are agentically operating in the same supply chain flow. They’re just doing it with agents buying; they’re not radically changing the way the process works. And that’s where the real edge exists.”

Not necessarily. Many of the new propositions depend on DSPs continuing to execute transactions, access inventory, and optimize within their own environments. The change may be who decides which platform gets the advertiser’s budget in the first place.

Milicevic maintained that “value always accrues to whoever is closest to the decision or outright owns the decision.”

Ergo, if DSPs cannot move upstream into genuine cross-channel decision-making, they risk becoming “dumb pipes,” i.e., a commoditized, essential infrastructure whose underlying value is less visible to buyers.

Incumbent DSPs, however, have established advertiser relationships, bidding technology, data integrations and scale. They could develop their own orchestration capabilities. A new intermediary would need to show that its independence produces better decisions, rather than simply adding another fee, according to several sources consulted for this piece.

Agencies already coordinate campaigns across channels, negotiate buying arrangements and manage the systems needed to execute them. Friedman, a former long-time agency-lead, argued that unified platforms could challenge agency order-management systems while bringing neglected channels, including creator-led media, into a common workflow.

Such a scenario would not eliminate strategic planning, creative development, or oversight, but it could change how much manual campaign administration agencies perform, where they develop proprietary expertise, and which services clients are willing to pay for, with AI at the center of many contractual renegotiations between advertisers and media agencies.

Such a scenario would not eliminate strategic planning, creative development, or oversight, but it could change how much manual campaign administration agencies perform, where they develop proprietary expertise, and which services clients are willing to pay for, with AI at the center of many contractual renegotiations between advertisers and media agencies.

The biggest obstacle may be the very platforms that independent systems are supposed to coordinate. Google, Meta, and Amazon control their own buying tools, data, and optimization systems. Access through an API does not necessarily provide the granular information or authority required to compare results and independently move budgets.

Trust is also an issue. A unified interface can display reports from multiple platforms without proving that the outcomes are comparable. Attribution windows, campaign objectives, and self-reported measurement differ across channels, meaning an independent buying layer needs a credible basis for deciding that one dollar is better spent in one environment than another.

Friedman was skeptical that interoperability would resolve the underlying problems, and highlighted how Google and Meta are unlikely to make their systems interoperable. He further questioned whether agencies could build the context and routing infrastructure needed to translate employees’ instructions into accurate buying decisions, even if every platform supported the Model Context Protocol. “Interoperability won’t solve anything,” he told Digiday.

Greg MacDonald, founder of Chelsea Strategies, said addressing media buying’s growing complexity could reduce spending on its mechanics and, over time, potentially improve advertiser outcomes. However, he remained cautious about how far unified platforms could go. “I’m skeptical that the walled gardens will give buyers more control than they have today,” he told Digiday, pointing to persistent difficulties with frequency capping across programmatic formats, let alone disconnected marketplaces.

“But you don’t have to solve every problem, and certainly not on day one to be useful,” MacDonald added. He compared the opportunity with programmatic guaranteed buying, which made the insertion-order process more efficient without fundamentally changing direct media buying.

Meanwhile, Milicevic said the more plausible near-term customer may be a multichannel advertiser between the largest global brands and small businesses that largely rely on a single platform. She also stressed the need for independent validation of results, rather than taking each platform’s own reporting at face value.

AI agents could help automate campaign setup, inventory discovery, negotiation, execution, and reporting. That makes agentic advertising relevant to the unified-platform debate, but not synonymous with it: automating tasks is different from making accountable, cross-channel budget decisions.

Unplugged Collective maps seven ways AI could change advertising (see graphic below). Per Weisberg, two are central to the unified buying debate: how agents decide where to spend and how they carry out those decisions across platforms.

Industry initiatives such as AdCP and IAB Tech Lab’s agentic work aim to establish ways for systems to communicate and execute workflows. Technical standards could reduce integration costs, but they cannot by themselves force walled gardens to disclose comparable data or surrender commercial control.

Seasoned industry observers have seen enough buzz phrases come and go to feel confident enough to make tongue-in-cheek observations when a new acronym becomes de rigueur among the industry’s chattering classes.

“I saw ‘UMP’ and I thought: ad network,” quipped Terence Kawaja, CEO, LUMA Partners, a prominent investment bank in the space, speaking on stage at this year’s Prebid Summit – channeling the industry adage, “everything is an ad network.”

Meanwhile, one prominent industry source, who requested anonymity to preserve industry relationships, dismissed the latest acronyms as another serving of “word salad.” However, the barbs, playful or otherwise, raise a palpable industry debate: As AI makes it cheaper to run campaigns across platforms, who gets to decide where the money goes?

The clearest test is whether advertisers entrust meaningful cross-channel allocation to an independent system — and can demonstrate that the decisions improve outcomes. Smaller-scale automation and campaign management do not, on their own, prove that a new market category has emerged.

Milicevic identified three indicators: real advertiser budgets moving under independent allocation, credible measurement outside any single walled garden, and changes in how marketing organizations structure teams. “If it’s truly structural, it’ll show up in the PNL, and if it’s slideware and fluff, then that’ll make its way into award entries and kind of the PR circuit,” she said.

For now, the most useful distinction is between controlling the infrastructure through which ads are purchased and controlling the decision about where the money goes. The latter is the more ambitious promise. Whether anyone can deliver it independently, at scale, and at a cost advertisers will accept remains unresolved.

Madison and Wall estimates creator marketing growing at double the pace of the rest of advertising, driven on by creator-led production investments.

#7235. Maker’s Mark spotlights signature red wax top in visual refresh

The brand evokes its heritage and craftsmanship with a new color palette, website and typography.

Maker’s Mark, a Suntory Global Spirits brand, pulled back the curtain on a new global brand identity this week that updates the brand’s color palette, photography and graphic elements with a more minimal, modern aesthetic that evokes the brand’s heritage. It is live in the U.S., China, Australia and the U.K., with other markets planned throughout 2027, according to a press release.

The new visual identity spotlights the bourbon’s iconic red wax. Custom typography is inspired by the handwriting of Margie Samuels, one of the co-founders of Maker’s Mark, who died in 1985 and was responsible for creating the brand name as well as its original bottle design. The bottle design remains unchanged, per press materials. Tactile elements included in the wax seal, such as wheat stalks and oak rings, were created using materials found at Star Hill Farm, one of the properties owned by the brand and the namesake of its Star Hill Farm Whisky.

"In a world increasingly driven by speed and automation, we still believe some things are worth making slowly, by hand," said Chloe Lloyd-Jones, vice president of global brand marketing at Maker's Mark, in press materials.

The brand last made changes to its visual identity in 2021, but they were not as extensive. The latest refresh was developed in partnership with Turner Duckworth London and is part of the “Perfectly Unreasonable” campaign.

The new design will appear across digital, social and retail channels, along with a new website. The overhaul is supported by in-person experiences in the U.S. and China, along with out-of-home activations in Australia and the U.K.

The refresh comes as the bourbon industry faces significant challenges, including slowing demand and a bloated inventory, which are driving down prices. Tariffs have also taken a toll on the industry, with exports of American whiskey, which includes bourbon, falling 19% in 2025. The industry’s issues are exacerbated by the general trend of Americans drinking less overall. A little over half of Americans said they drink, according to a recent Gallup poll, the lowest it’s been since the organization started measuring the trend in 1939.

Suntory Global Spirits ceased production at its flagship Jim Beam distillery in Clermont, Kentucky at the beginning of 2026. It recently announced it would continue to pause distilling operations until at least the end of next year. Maker’s Mark is distilled at a different location, which remains operational.

“Perfectly Unreasonable” launched in 2025 and is the first campaign for the brand by Carmichael Lynch. In December, Maker’s Mark entered into its first sports league sponsorship and became the first official spirits partner of the women’s basketball league Unrivaled.

Suntory Beverage & Food Limited, which is traded on the Tokyo Stock Exchange, saw its revenue increase 9.8% year-over-year for the nine months ending Sept. 30, 2026, according to financial results.

#7231. Brands booking creators to make their ads are pushing up $116B global influencer market

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

Brand spending on creator marketing is set to grow twice as fast as spending on advertising as a whole over the next five years, according to advertising research firm Madison & Wall.

The consultancy estimated that creator spending will rise 12% each year until 2030, versus a 6% increase in ad spending each year between 2025 and 2030. In 2026, the researcher estimates that marketers will spend $116 billion on creator activity.

Driving that growth is ad spend intended to boost or promote creator content to a larger audience — which accounted for 44%, or $52 billion, of global creator marketing spending in 2026 — and brands paying creators to produce advertising assets for use in CTV, display and paid social contexts.

The latter accounted for 12% of global creator spending in 2026, but will rise 16% each year until the end of the decade, making it the fastest-growing segment of the creator economy. Traditional sponsored posts or ambassador relationships accounted for 28% of creator spending.

Brands attempting to reach large audiences on social platforms have begun to look to creators not just as a distribution point, but as a source of creative production that can meet the need for a diverse set of creative assets at a lower price point than traditional agency methods.

Unilever CEO Fernando Fernandez, for example, recently said that the CPG giant’s social content had to be constantly refreshed to prevent it from going stale. “The lifespan of a video today is four days,” he said during the firm’s recent Capital Markets Day. “Content has to be renovated because after four days you don’t have diminishing levels of return, you have negative marginal return.”

“The reason we see brands engaging with this is just because it’s cheaper,” said Luke Stillman, managing director at Madison & Wall. “Everybody is trying to squeeze down all those non-working expenses as much as possible.”

As creator rosters get larger — Unilever now works with around 500,000 — the need to manage such programs has driven spending on specialist agencies, platforms and talent representation. Spending on creator intermediaries (fees for specialist agencies, platforms, or talent representation) accounted for 8% of global creator spending.

Stillman said he expected to see spending on that slice grow further in coming years. “Identifying the right creators, contracting with all those creators, managing non-delivery and legal issues and payment issues are really complex, and so there’s plenty of space for ancillary services there,” he said.

“We’re seeing an increase on budgets year-on-year from our clients,” said Andy Ryan, co-founder at social and influencer agency Komodo. Ryan estimated client spending on creator marketing had increased 20%-30% in 2026.

Olivia Wedderburn, head of influence at Born Social, saw an even bigger jump, estimating that client spending on creator activity had risen 50% compared with last year. “I would anticipate that will only continue to grow quite heavily,” she said.

The U.S. market accounted for 43% of creator spending last year, per Madison & Wall estimates, while the U.K. and Germany are the next-largest, at 5% and 4% respectively. The U.S. will see spending grow 15% this year. India was the fastest growing market, at 20%.

Madison & Wall’s estimate for asset-led spending covered several related practices: whitelisting, fees for user-generated content, content licensing, and fees for producing creative assets.

However brands are commissioning it, creator-led production is on the rise. “More of our brand partners are bringing creators in as the production engine for their paid media, not just their organic social,” said Nicholas Spiro, chief commercial officer at Viral Nation.

While cost is a major factor, as Stillman noted, agency marketers also believe creator-made work can engage audiences more effectively than an ad made for the same environment by a creative director.

“Creator-made assets simply connect best with potential customers on a human level,” said Spiro. “They tend to drive performance better than traditional brand creative, and they can be more measurable for certain objectives than organic creator-only content.”

While creator marketing was once dominated by apparel and beauty brands, healthcare, automotive and finance advertisers now spend on creator activity too.

“Where some sectors like fashion and beauty have been on this for a number of years, and therefore they’re already doing a fantastic job at it… some sectors [were] behind and they’re catching up and are now throwing more at it,” said Ryan.

“An ad can make a claim. A parent can prove it,” said Carmen Graham, CMO at Genexa, a youth-targeted healthcare brand.

“Parent creators can also do what we can’t: show the product in real use, in their home. The messier and less art-directed the medicine cabinet, the more we see ourselves and our customer in the content,” she said.

“Our bet is that this commitment to being parent-first will drive the trust this category requires — especially if you’re switching to a new product to support you in the most dire moments of parenthood.”

A new crop of companies promise to coordinate advertising budgets across platforms, but can they control buying decisions, or merely add another interface to the existing system?

16% — Walt Disney Co. revenue growth for the fiscal fourth quarter ended Oct. 3, inflated more than 7 percentage points by a 53rd week in the current fiscal year. Earnings per share also grew 16%, even adjusting out that 53rd week effect.

$3 billion — Total annual cost-cutting savings run rate Disney expect by fiscal 2029 through layoffs, procurement and other efficiencies.

24% — Increase in aggregated viewership of Monday Night Football through the first nine weeks of the NFL season, a good sign heading into Disney’s first-ever Super Bowl broadcast.

Expansion of the NCAA College Football Playoff to 24 teams, and from 11 to 23 games next year, promises an even bigger sports rights earnings take for Disney. “ESPN is going to have exclusive rights to all the rounds,” D’Amaro said. “We’ve got broad distribution flexibility across ABC and ESPN.”

The CEO declined to disclose the rights costs, and acknowledged that Disney has sub-licensing rights, but didn’t commit to any plans to get outside networks involved.

“Changes like these are not easy, and they affect people who care deeply about Disney and have contributed so much to this company. At the same time, these steps are important if we want to be a company that moves faster, adapts to a rapidly changing environment, and keeps investing in the content and technology that will drive our growth. We make these choices because we believe so strongly in what Disney can be.” —Disney CEO Josh D’Amaro on impending layoffs

#7218. Senior Forward Deployed Engineer at Clutch

Clutch builds AI agents that work on behalf of credit unions. Emma, our collections agent, is live at five credit unions and handled 60,000+ member conversations in her first twelve weeks at one of them. Behind her is a growing portfolio: origination, fraud step-up, member (re)engagement and activation agents, all built on the same Agents Platform (TypeScript, NestJS, our own agent SDK, MCP, Temporal, promptfoo).

As a FDE you are one of the two engineers on an agent. You take it from "sold" to "live and performing" at a specific credit union: you sit in the discovery sessions, you build the integrations to their core banking and loan systems, you author and tune the agent's behavior, you run the live tests with their staff, and you stay with it through go-live and the first weeks of production. When something needs a person in the room, you are the one who flies.

This is a senior engineering role, not a solutions or support role. You write production code in our monorepo every week: integrations, modules, and the agents themselves (prompts, tools, evals), together with the other engineers on the pod. The difference from a traditional engineer is that you are on site more often, you make judgment calls in front of the customer, and you own the outcome. What you learn in the field flows straight back into the product: defaults, playbooks, and the platform capabilities the next agent will need.

You build; our AI Operations Managers deliver. The pod (pod lead plus engineers) designs and builds each agent in a high-touch way with the first customers. Once the agent is defined and live, AI Ops coordinates the implementations, configuration and ongoing success at the next credit unions. You hand them something that works and a playbook to run it.

This role requires roughly 20% of travel on average, with one- to two-week stretches around kickoffs and go-lives. Based in the US, able to travel on short notice

You'll join the Agents team, a small, senior group operating as a startup inside Clutch: seven engineers, a dedicated AI product group, and AI sales specialists, working directly with some of the largest credit unions in the country. Every agent is staffed by a pair of engineers, one leaning toward the conversation and LLM side, one toward software and integrations, with a pod lead who owns the product day to day. There is no layer between you and the customer, and no layer between you and the platform team.

The operating style is direct, written, and evidence-driven: BLUF updates, evals over opinions, business outcomes over ticket counts. The team is spread across Brazil, Argentina and the US and works async by default. Being on site is a tool we use at specific moments (kickoff, live testing, go-live), not a permanent posting.

Map where the current implementation process loses time and where a default or a tool would have removed a round trip

Contribute at least one reusable capability back to the Agents Platform that the next implementation will not have to rebuild

Have taken one net-new agent from sold to live at its first credit union, with data you can show the partner

#7213. A Crash Course in Marketing to Machines

Some years ago, one of us, Jim Lecinski, wrote a manifesto called the “Zero Moment of Truth,” or “ZMOT,” which argued that before the traditional point of purchase arrives — before a customer reaches the shampoo aisle shelf or the car dealer’s lot -— that customer goes looking for evidence to help them decide what products are the most credible and therefore what to buy.

Many marketers still begin the marketing process from the company out: Who are we? What do we want to sell? What message can we create to persuade people to buy? In effect, they are working their way from the product to the customer. Great marketers, however, work the other way around.

They begin by asking what job the customer is trying to get done, how that customer moves through the purchase journey, what questions arise along the way, and which sources shape confidence and choice. Then they align their go-to-market accordingly.

Digital marketing has made the customer decision process more complex. Rather than relying on a single message or source, customers consult multiple sources before deciding what to buy. They gather evidence. They search, compare, read what other customers say, watch experts and creators, and increasingly ask AI assistants for answers and recommendations.

The original three sources – search engines, social conversations, and ratings and reviews – remain important. But two others now deserve a place in the ZMOT framework: content creators and AI assistants. Both influence how customers evaluate choices, form preferences, and make purchase decisions.

And these sources also interact. Search results show online community discussions, reviews, and creator videos. Creator content influences people and AI-generated answers. AI assistants draw from information created across the web, synthesize it, and increasingly build or narrow the consideration set on the customer’s behalf.

The Zero Moment of Truth provides the clearest framework for understanding this change.

The concept of the Zero Moment remains as relevant today as when it was introduced: The Zero Moment of Truth is the critical pre-purchase period when customers seek information, compare options, reduce uncertainty, and form preferences. A stimulus creates awareness or activates a need. Before the traditional point of purchase arrives, the customer goes looking for evidence that will help them decide what to believe and what to choose.

What continues to evolve are the sources customers use to gather the information that shapes this decision-making zero moment.

Content Creators help customers understand categories, interpret differences, see products in context, and decide with confidence. AI assistants increasingly answer questions, compare alternatives, form shortlists, analyze options, and recommend specific brands.

The expansion of avenues from which customers acquire knowledge, requires marketers to rethink their approach. A customer considering a new suitcase may move among search, online community discussions, ratings & reviews, content creator videos, and an AI chatbot all during a single decision journey. These five sources therefore form an interconnected portfolio rather than separate or sequential channels.

Therefore, winning the Zero Moment still requires clear and persuasive brand messages across these various avenues. The marketer’s mandate now extends beyond the messages the brand creates and distributes. The expanded Zero Moment requires marketers to move from managing brand messages alone to managing the broader body of decision evidence that customers and AI systems use to understand, evaluate, and choose brands. Each source has a distinct role:

These roles overlap, which is precisely why marketers must manage them together. Creator videos appear in search results. Online community conversations influence both human perceptions and AI-generated answers. Reviews shape search visibility, customer trust, and machine-generated comparisons. Brand content can support every source when it is useful, clear, current, and corroborated by evidence outside the brand.

Notice that AI assistants differ from the other four sources because they play two roles. They are a fifth source customers consult directly, and they synthesize information drawn from the other four. This becomes even more important as AI is directly embedded across categories, including social, ecommerce and productivity.

An AI-generated answer may incorporate search results, brand websites, publishers, social conversations, reviews, creator content, and other third-party evidence. AI increasingly assembles and interprets the consideration set on the customer’s behalf. It can identify contradictions, surface recurring criticisms, compare claims, and omit brands that lack sufficient credible evidence from the answers or recommendations it provides. In fact, Google recently introduced new ad formats including Conversational Discovery Ads, Direct Offers, and Highlighted Ads, which answer questions, supply analysis and offer suggestions right inside the ad creative.

Strong search rankings no longer guarantee visibility in AI-generated answers. A brand may perform well in search engines but be missing from the recommendations from AI assistants or overviews. It may have a polished website while customers, creators, and reviewers tell a different story elsewhere. It may appear across all five sources with inconsistent product information, claims, pricing, or positioning.

Each source can strengthen or weaken the evidence found in the others.

Marketers still need persuasive messages, which are an essential part of the evidence customers encounter.

Marketers have traditionally focused on the messages they create and distribute. Today, they also must understand and influence the evidence customers encounter elsewhere, including reviews and creator content and AI-generated recommendations.

Decision evidence is the accumulated body of information, experiences, claims, reviews, demonstrations, conversations, and third-party validation that shapes what customers and AI systems understand and believe about a brand.

This evidence includes useful and authoritative brand content, accurate product information, strong customer experiences, current ratings and reviews, credible third-party coverage, informed creator content, and consistent facts that both humans and machines can understand. YouTube becomes a valuable source of authority, based on the way large language models prioritize multimodal content and direct video citations. A recent BrightEdge report indicates that a brand’s YouTube presence represents a unique path to AI optimization. As the dominant video platform in AI search, YouTube is already capturing nearly 30% of AI citations in Google’s AI Overviews (source).

While brands cannot fully control every source they can influence the quality, availability, credibility, and coherence of the evidence customers encounter. The goal is to ensure that the evidence available across all five sources supports the position the brand wants to earn in the customer’s mind.

To help marketers see how effectively their brand appears during the Zero Moment of Truth we’ve developed a helpful Zero Moment Five-Source Evidence Check.

Start with the three to five questions that matter most in the customer’s decision. These might include:

Then examine what a customer would find about the brand across search engines, social conversations, ratings and reviews, content creators, and AI assistants. For each source, assess five things:

Record your brand’s results for each source as Strong, Weak, or Absent (See Fig 2). The weak and absent cells reveal the most important evidence gaps.

With the completed diagnostic in hand, the next step is to close the gaps. Prioritize the weak or absent areas most likely to influence customer choice, assign clear owners and actions, and strengthen the underlying evidence through better content, corrected information, stronger customer experiences, review management, creator engagement, or credible third-party validation.

Then repeat the check regularly. The goal is to move every priority area toward Strong and keep it there as customer behavior, platform results, and AI-generated answers continue to change.

The expanded Zero Moment requires marketers to move from managing brand messages alone to managing the decision evidence customers and AI systems use to understand, evaluate, and choose brands.

The challenge for marketers is no longer about getting the right message in front of the right customer. It’s ensuring that when customers – or AI agents – look for evidence, they find information that is accurate and credible while also maintaining a consistent brand promise.

Jim Lecinski is a Clinical Professor of Marketing at Northwestern University's Kellogg School of Management where he has won multiple prestigious teaching awards including Kellogg Professor of the Year. His career includes twelve years at Google, where he was vp of customer solutions for the Americas. Lecinski's seminal book “Winning the Zero Moment of Truth” has been read by over 300,000 marketers, and his latest project, “The AI Marketing Canvas: A Five Step AI Plan for Marketers” has been named both a top AI book and a top business book.

Kelly Cutler brings together industry experience and academic expertise in her role as Clinical Associate Professor at Northwestern University, where she teaches marketing and communication across multiple schools and programs. She also serves as Associate Director of Research at the Spiegel Research Center, focusing on AI integration and the evolving digital marketing landscape. Kelly is regularly featured in both academic and industry publications and speaks at conferences and events around the world. She remains actively engaged with industry, advising organizations and delivering executive education and professional development programs to global audiences.

#7205. Amazon’s New Christmas Ad Is a Bet Against Itself

Editor’s note: Brave Bison, which acquired Mark Ritson’s MiniMBA in 2025, owns about 28% of System1 and sought to acquire the rest this year.

Joy is stuck at the airport on Christmas Eve, the snow coming down hard, and every flight cancelled. So she picks up her bag of presents and sets off for home the long way: a bus, a lift from some donkey farmers, a night in a boat cabin on a heaving sea, a taxi driven by a giant. At every stop, she thanks a stranger with one of the Amazon parcels meant for her family.

This is “Joy Delivered”, Amazon’s new holiday ad, which launched earlier this month and runs like a proper movie. A new version of the Beatles’ “Golden Slumbers” does a lot of the emotional heavy lifting and the hug at the end will get you.

There are two problems with the ad, however. One is peripheral. One central.

The peripheral one first. Amazon filmed its white Christmas in New Zealand, in and around Dunedin, to be specific. But it’s summer down there, and it will be even warmer by Christmas, when most of the country will be at the beach in a pair of shorts.

The central problem is bigger. Amazon already has a great Christmas ad: “Joy Ride.”

Three old ladies sitting on a snowy hill watch the local kids go sledding. One of them orders some seat cushions from Amazon. The box turns up and the next thing you know they are flying down the hill and, for a few seconds, they are girls again. There are no words. Just an instrumental of “In My Life.”

“Joy Ride” first ran in 2023 and got the maximum 5.8 stars from System1. Basically perfection.

Then Amazon did something very unusual for a big marketer. Last year, instead of making a new ad, it ran the old one again. It scored 5.7, again putting it in the rare company of less than 1% of all ads made all year.

None of this should surprise anyone. Coca-Cola first ran “Holidays Are Coming” in 1995. The trucks have rolled out every Christmas since, and System1 says the ad always scores five stars in its testing. When System1 looked at more than 50,000 ads, it found that scores barely move as ads get older, and that some of the very best actually improve as people get to know them.

Ads don’t wear out, at least not for consumers.

Amazon’s problem is that its advertising decisions are made by marketers — people who like to make new, shiny stuff. Jo Shoesmith, Amazon’s global chief brand officer, told ADWEEK that people want nostalgia but also “something they haven’t seen before.”

The average brand building campaign reaches its maximum impact about two to three years into execution. Seasonal stuff can often, as Coke has proved, live on for decades. But the average marketer is tired of their ad, and wants to make something new.

It’s the same story with slogans, with logos, with distinctive brand assets. Marketers forget they are not the consumer. They look at their ads for at least 9,000 hours before they release it into the wild, and are over it before most of the target market has noticed it.

There is an intermediate position. Amazon could pull a Coca-Cola: bring out their old ad every year but also make a crappy, inferior new execution alongside it. But why create a bat to beat yourself with? Better to remove the outstanding, already made, already free, already proven, already much-loved Christmas ad completely, and then run something less good, with lots more risk and cost attached. Makes perfect sense.

We read all the time that marketers need more support from their CEO, more budget, more seats in the boardroom.

But what they mostly need is patience. It takes years, not months, for a good campaign to work. Amazon knew that last Christmas, but it has already forgotten it.

Mark Ritson has a PhD in Marketing and spent 25 years working as a marketing professor, and has also worked as both a global brand consultant and as the in-house brand consultant for LVMH. His articles have appeared in the Sloan Management Review, Harvard Business Review, the Journal of Advertising and the Journal of Consumer Research.

Select ADWEEK as one of your favorite sources and stay up to date on our latest content.

Regularly review customer environments and usage patterns to identify scalability, performance, reliability, security, and compliance risks and opportunities.

Coordinate across Support and Engineering on complex or high-impact issues. TAMs are not frontline support, but provide the context, prioritization, and follow-through that drive resolution, root cause analysis, and preventative action.

Serve as the voice of the customer to Engineering and Product, sharing specific, reproducible feedback on recurring pain points and taking part in beta and early access programs.

Partner with Sales and Customer Success on account health and retention, with technical input on renewals, expansions, and evaluations, and early flags on risks to satisfaction.

6+ years of relevant work experience in engineering, SRE, technical consulting, or similar, including operating production systems under real constraints.

Strong technical depth across database internals, scalability, security posture, and infrastructure tradeoffs. You understand a customer’s architecture as well as they do, without escalating every question.

A proactive mindset: you spot risks and optimization opportunities before the customer does, and you treat your accounts like a personal portfolio.

Sound escalation judgment: you know when to escalate, when to resolve internally, and how to communicate clearly under pressure without creating noise.

Very strong communication skills. TAMs explain complex issues to non-technical executives and root causes to senior engineers, often in the same day.

Awareness of how TAM performance shows up in retention and NRR, including surfacing expansion signals and renewal risks early.

Experience working in async or globally distributed teams.

We hire globally. We believe you can do your best work from anywhere. There are no Supabase offices, but we provide a WeWork membership or co-working allowance you can use anywhere in the world.

Every team member receives ESOP (equity ownership) in the company. We want everyone to share in the upside of what we’re building together.

#7168. 5 Questions For …MS NOW Executive Producers On One Year Anniversary Of Name Change

On November 15, 2025, Versant switched the name of its news network from MSNBC to MS NOW. The new name, which stands for My Source for News, Opinion and the World, had to build alot of its infrastructure from scratch after detaching from NBC News.

The switch to a new name did not affect viewership, as the network ranked second among all cable networks in total viewers during the third quarter. In addition, in the past year it launched its direct-to-consumer platform, MS NOW Membership.

Some of the people who have witnessed all these changes up close are the executive producers of MS NOW’s linear shows. They have been responsible for establishing the new name in their programs as well as ensuring the mission remains the same even after the transition.

In recognition of MS NOW’s first anniversary, TVNewser spoke to five executive producers, who launched new shows within the first year. They Include, State of Play with Peter Alexander’s Sandi Cohen, On the Line with Alicia Menendez’s Marti Hause, The 11th Hour with Ali Velshi’s Rebekah Dryden, Connect with Jacob Soboroff’s Dina Moss, and Joy Fowlin from The Assignment with Antonia Hylton.

(This interview has been edited for length)

TVNewser: How has the first year known as MS NOW been?

Cohen: I’ve only been here for the last six months, but the energy is unmatched. There’s a feeling of positivity and building towards the future, and a real team effort to do so. It’s been inspiring and given me a boost.

Hause: It has been a really exciting year at MS NOW. It’s been remarkable to see this entire organization build from the ground up, driving toward the ultimate goal of serving our viewers.

Dryden: I think of the first year as MS NOW as a year of proving it, a series of rapid-fire, real-time proof points of who we are and what we can do as a network, standing up on our own.

Moss: When MS NOW first relaunched, I never would have imagined we’d be here. It’s been the greatest honor and challenge to launch Connect alongside Jacob and our team.

Fowlin: Two words to describe the first year as MS NOW: seamless and exciting. Change can be hard, but everyone worked together to make the transition as smooth as possible. It was truly a testament of leading from the top.

TN: What does the new network name signify for you and your program?

Cohen: We’re meeting the moment! “NOW” is right in the name. A constant reminder of why we do what we do, and to meet that mission.

Hause: The new name signifies to me that we are covering the day’s news with hosts, reporters and guests who bring a depth of expertise and experience to help our viewers navigate the stakes of what we are seeing.

Dryden: “News, Opinion, World” has always been our work. But I especially love what “My Source” says about who we’ve become. By 11 o’clock, people have had the whole day’s news thrown at them. Being someone’s source means answering that with facts they can trust, and then sending them to bed with something to hold onto.

Moss: We hope that our audience sees us as their ‘source’ for connection and community. What we’ve really been prioritizing on Connect are voices and stories that our viewers can relate to.

Fowlin: MS NOW stands for accuracy, precision, and collaboration. I am so proud to work with a team and an entire network of people who truly enjoy coming to work every day.

TN: As the leaders of new programs on MS NOW, how has it gone so far?

Cohen: We’re having a blast. Nearly every day Peter tells me how fun doing this show is. And he’s right. We have a wonderful team of smart and kind producers who work incredibly hard.

Hause: It has been such an invigorating process. Starting a new show has been a total reset on how we cover the news of the day. For On The Line, it’s a constant discussion about how we focus on the stakes of any given story, not the odds. Dryden: It has been a joy to lead a new chapter of The 11th Hour with Ali Velshi. We both feel so privileged to have been given the opportunity to take up the 11th Hour mantle and trusted with the freedom to make it our own.

Moss: Long nights, early mornings, and a ton of coffee! But the most fun I’ve had in my career.

Fowlin: Launching The Assignment has been one of the best opportunities of my career. I had never built a show from scratch, and what a privilege it was to do. I inherited an incredible team of producers who embraced the show’s vision.

TN: What similarities have you seen between relaunching MS NOW and starting a new program?

Cohen: Our show has had to find our voice, our people, and our mission. It takes a lot of trial and error. Leaders across the company are doing the same thing. Whether that’s for our digital products, in-person experiences, or linear TV.

Hause: I see a lot of similarities in the way we’ve questioned conventional wisdom and our processes. It’s led to some great changes to the status quo and brought new opportunities for members of our team.

Dryden: Cable news producers have always been a scrappy and industrious group, and there was a real sense of building something together as we launched this new show and this new network. So every challenge felt like a team effort and, in the end, an accomplishment.

Moss: What I’ve enjoyed so much is the collaboration. When we relaunched MS NOW, there was collective effort to make it a success. I feel the same about launching Connect with Jacob Soboroff. It’s been a melding of minds (and some of the brightest minds in the industry, at that).

Fowlin: Yes, putting together a new show does require a start-up mentality. You need grit, drive, flexibility, and a good dose of fearlessness. We all come together with the same mission: to put together a news program centered on the human stories behind the headlines.

TN: What’s one interesting nugget you can share about your anchor?

Cohen: Peter Alexander does not drink coffee. Even when we’re traveling and working long days, there is no caffeine, and he really doesn’t need it.

Hause: She just planned her 8th-grade class reunion.

Dryden: I think Ali has a reputation for being very serious. But behind the scenes, he doesn’t take himself seriously – he’s just really funny and warm..

Moss: Jacob will respond to viewers on social media during commercial breaks. And sometimes, if you tell him to slip a random word into the newscast…he just might do it.

Fowlin: Antonia is an incredibly hard-working anchor and reporter. And she’s also a dedicated Real Housewives fan. When we’re not talking about story ideas or guest requests, we’re texting about whatever wild and crazy thing happened on the latest episode.

Mark Mwachiro is a TVNewser contributor who has been writing for the blog since March 2022. Based out of New York, Mark has also contributed to ADWEEK.

Select ADWEEK as one of your favorite sources and stay up to date on our latest content.

The Five was the No.1 program of the evening

The news broke while Squawk on the Street was on air

#7161. GTA VI Shouldn’t Be Your Brand’s Only Gaming Strategy

As our industry counts down to the launch of Grand Theft Auto VI, I reflect back to a moment that shaped how I think about gaming. I was at E3 2001 and was given the opportunity to get hands-on with Grand Theft Auto III. I’d played GTA 2 on PC a couple years earlier, so I understood the franchise, but what I was seeing now was something else entirely. A true, fully realized 3D open world. A city that felt populated, alive, and full of possibility. Characters with personality. And gameplay opportunities around every corner. Rockstar’s vision was unmistakable.

At the time, I was marketing the Pokémon Trading Card Game at Wizards of the Coast, building on my previous gaming experience at GameWorks, before leaving the tabletop business to join the Madden NFL franchise at EA SPORTS, where I eventually became Vice President of Brand Marketing. My time at EA taught me something fundamental: the connections that brands build between games and their fanbases are powerful. Gamers represent a blue ocean, an enormous, engaged, and largely untapped audience for brands.

Grand Theft Auto VI will be a culturally destabilizing event, a game that will reshape the gaming market, where all cultural gravity will be focused on a single product in which all other forms of entertainment will imitate. It is expected to easily outperform the former biggest entertainment launch of all time, Avengers: Endgame, and make more than the combined box office revenue of Avengers: Doomsday and Dune: Part Three.

Grand Theft Auto VI is the tip of the spear for console gaming’s resurgence. Industry data partner Circana recently reported physical video game software unit sales up 40% year-over-year, with digital marketplaces setting new concurrent user records almost every month. There are now 150 million new consoles in the market, and more games being released for them than ever before. Gaming has continued to extend its lead as the dominant global entertainment segment.

With all of this momentum we’re seeing a ton of brands dipping their toe in the gaming market right now, attempting to recreate the aesthetic of Grand Theft Auto VI, trying to be a part of the conversation. But Grand Theft Auto VI isn’t a fit for brands that are used to risk-adverse advertising campaigns. And truthfully, Rockstar doesn’t need a brand marketing partner. When the entire world is expected to buy your game on day one, there’s nothing a brand could offer that moves the needle. Brands are better off collaborating with gaming properties that are seeking support and fit their cultural ethos, joining those in the gaming space who have already figured this out.

For those looking to figure out how to authentically market to gamers, there’s already a fair number of successful case studies we’ve been a part of.

The beauty brand OPI consistently explores ways of engaging with new audiences. Women make up 50% of the video game audience but remain underserved in the market. They worked with Xbox to come up with a colorful partnership: a nail polish line aimed directly at female gamers, which saw immediate sellouts, backed by an experiential campaign in select Target stores. The program’s success led to a second activation the following year that expanded beyond beauty to include the Sunkissed Vibes OPI Special Edition Xbox Controller.

IKEA recently tackled a different segment entirely: the physical environment where gaming happens. Both brands’ top designers collaborated on a collection focused on the gamer’s space, solving problems they’re really good at: cord clutter, controller posture, and adaptable living setups. Its popularity demonstrated how a brand can find an original, authentic way to connect with gamers by genuinely improving their experience.

Fanta found a rare opening in the beverage category that fit their evolving entertainment strategy. They partnered with Xbox as an entry point to bring gamers their delicious Fanta and exclusive in-game rewards. Fanta continued their reputation of going big, and this one included promotional cans being distributed in over sixty countries. Each can was wrapped in exclusive artwork and the collaboration featured an entirely new flavor, Fanta Crimson Sour Cherry. The activation saw over a billion online impressions before a single dollar of media spend was used, a credit to the enthusiasm both fanbases have for their products.

Trolli entered the space after their data revealed that gaming was their audience’s biggest hobby. We helped them build a long-term strategy anchored by exclusive giveaways through unique CPG collaborations and League Trolli, an ongoing esports program that connected them with gamers through competition and prizes. This created an always-on consistency that formed an authentic, reliable relationship with their core demographic.

These successful activations within the gaming community require a complete understanding of the gaming experience. Brands succeed when they move beyond superficial advertising and instead deliver tangible value that resonates with gaming audiences, earning organic buzz and fan enthusiasm as a result. Finding an authentic point of connection with gamers through a brand’s existing strengths creates the inflection point of success. Brands should offer genuine value, whether that’s representation, utility, exclusivity, or sustained engagement, in a way that feels true to both the brands and gaming community.

Grand Theft Auto V shipped in 2013 and has gone on to sell 230 million copies. In the thirteen years since, we’ve watched pioneering brands enter mainstream gaming and find enormous success, while others have yet to figure out their own true path. Brands can’t be a tourist in the gaming space. Don’t let another thirteen years pass before creating a strategy for the biggest entertainment market in the world.

Chris Erb is the head of gaming at CAA Brand Consulting, a division of leading entertainment and sports agency Creative Artists Agency (CAA). Based in Los Angeles, Erb brings expertise across traditional gaming, entertainment, and culture. He oversees potential client opportunities and gaming strategies, working collaboratively across the division to ensure the best teams and expertise are brought forward. Erb has worked with gaming clients, including Microsoft, Bethesda, CD Projekt Red, and Sega, as well as traditional brands, including WK Kellogg’s, Doritos, and Fanta. Prior to CAA Brand Consulting, Erb worked at Electronic Arts, where he led its Madden NFL franchise and EA SPORTS brand.

The marketing vet has a history of brokering major sports sponsorship deals, including Pepsi's decade-long reign over the Super Bowl Halftime Show

Most Kit creators don't have a dedicated Creator Growth Manager, and when their emails start landing in spam, they need someone who can find the cause quickly and explain it in plain language. We're looking for a Deliverability and Compliance Specialist to own deliverability support for that long tail of creators, run Kit's abuse desk, and make the compliance calls that protect the sending reputation every creator on Kit shares.

You'll also build the playbooks, training, and AI-assisted workflows that help Support and contractors handle more deliverability questions on their own, and you'll partner with our infrastructure engineers as we run more of our sending in-house. This is a full-time individual contributor role for someone who likes digging through headers and bounce logs to find the real cause of a problem, can explain DMARC alignment to a first-time creator without jargon, and is comfortable making a firm call when a situation is gray. Our Deliverability team: a Deliverability Lead who supports our highest-value accounts and leads deliverability education, and a Deliverability Specialist who onboards and ramps up large senders moving to Kit

Our infrastructure engineers, who run Kit's sending infrastructure

Our compliance and anti-abuse engineers, who build the automated checks that keep spammers off Kit

Product Specialists and the Support team, who handle the front line and escalate deliverability issues to you

Customer Success Managers (we call them Creator Growth Managers at Kit), Sales and Migrations, and Product Managers

#7148. Senior Analytics Engineer at Kit

Kit is the email marketing platform experts choose to grow their audience, deepen their relationships, and build a business around the work they love. For coaches, YouTubers, authors, podcasters, and other creatives, there isn't a better marketing hub to rely on to grow audiences, automate email marketing, and sell digital products — all within one platform.More importantly, there isn't a team more committed to helping creators earn a living. Our Sales team needs a dedicated data partner, and creator commerce (how creators monetize by selling digital products) is becoming a bigger part of how Kit and our creators scale. Kit is growing, so we're looking for a Senior Analytics Engineer to own Kit's sales and commerce data from end to end.

Accurate sales data gives our team a clear picture of the pipeline and the confidence to make informed decisions. That’s what makes this role so important.

Your primary focus will be Sales: the dbt models that turn our CRM data into trusted pipeline, deal, and funnel metrics, and the Omni dashboards our sales leader and Senior Revenue Operations Manager use to run the business. You'll also model creator commerce data, so we understand what creators sell and earn through Kit, and you'll pick up reporting work with our Product team, such as feature usage reporting. On a small data team, we step outside our usual lanes when the need is there, so the mix will shift over time.

You'll build for two kinds of consumers: people and AI. Teammates increasingly answer their own questions by querying our semantic layer through Omni's AI and MCP connections to AI tools. That means your models, metric definitions, and documentation need to be clear and correct enough for an AI agent to use them without a data teammate in the loop.

This is a full-time individual contributor role for someone who likes being close to the business, enjoys turning messy operational data into numbers people trust, and is happy to work on both the models and the dashboards that sit on top of them. When Sales and Product have clear, reliable data, we help more creators build more valuable businesses with Kit.

You'll report to Sam Umachi, Head of Data (Linkedin).

Sales and RevOps are your primary partners, who will rely on the models and reporting you own. You'll also partner with our Product team on reporting for features like Visual Automations and on how creators use Kit.

Within our data team, you'll work alongside a Staff Analytics Engineer who owns org-wide semantic layer governance and modeling standards, a Senior Data Engineer who owns ingestion and pipelines, and a Senior Business Analytics Partner focused on revenue opportunities. This role has no direct reports.

First Week: Work through our onboarding curriculum in Notion and meet the data team, our Senior Revenue Operations Manager, the sales team, and your product partners. Get set up in our core tools: dbt Cloud, GitHub, Amazon Redshift, Omni, HubSpot, Linear, Hightouch and Slack. Set up your AI-assisted development environment and learn the shared AI workflows our data team uses. Open and merge your first small pull request so you learn our review and deployment flow early.

First Month: Map how HubSpot objects (deals, pipelines and stages, companies, contacts, and owners) flow into our dbt models, and document the gaps. Take over day-to-day ownership of the sales models and Omni dashboards that teammates have been covering. Audit current sales reporting and list where definitions differ or numbers don't reconcile. Get familiar with how commerce data (products creators sell and the revenue they earn through Kit) lands in the warehouse today. Agree with RevOps and Sales on the most important questions they need answered. Review teammates' pull requests and contribute to our shared SQL, dbt, and documentation standards. Share a written reflection with the team on what you've learned and where you'd focus first.

First Six Months: Ship and maintain well-tested, documented dbt models for core sales metrics, such as pipeline, deal velocity, stage conversion, win rates, and the sales-led funnel. Expose those metrics through the Omni semantic layer, working within the team's governance standards. Rebuild or refresh the core sales dashboards so pipeline reviews and forecasting conversations run on numbers that don't need manual reconciliation. Resolve competing sales metric definitions by writing down the options and tradeoffs, aligning with Sales and RevOps, and documenting the outcome. Model creator-level commerce data, such as products sold, revenue earned through Kit, and conversion, in partnership with data engineering. Write semantic layer descriptions, metric definitions, and documentation that let Omni's AI and MCP-connected AI tools answer routine sales questions correctly, and test them against real questions from Sales, RevOps and Product. Get Sales to the point where routine questions are answered through self-serve, whether in Omni dashboards, Omni's AI, or AI tools connected over MCP, rather than one-off requests.

First Year: Be the recognized owner of Kit's sales data, the person Sales and RevOps go to first and trust without second-guessing. Give our revenue analysis work the sales and commerce models it depends on, so deeper questions about pipeline health and creator earnings can be answered quickly, thereby enabling us to make creators more successful. Leave your domains well documented and well tested, legible to new teammates, stakeholders, and the AI agents that query our semantic layer. Help raise the team's modeling quality through code review, pairing, and sharing what you learn.

Deep dbt experience. You build modular, tested, documented dbt projects, and you're comfortable with incremental models, snapshots, macros, CI, and scheduled jobs in dbt Cloud or similar.

Advanced SQL. You reason carefully about grain, joins, and window functions, and you write queries that are readable and performant on a cloud warehouse.

Data modeling for sales and GTM. You can model deals, pipeline stages and their history, ownership changes, and funnel conversion into clean, reusable structures. You are opinionated on Kimball standards and when a concept should be a fact or dimension.

CRM data fluency. You know how sales data behaves in a CRM, including stage changes, backdated edits, and custom properties. HubSpot is preferred, but other CRM experience (e.g. Salesforce) applies just as well. We care most that you recognize common CRM patterns.

AI-assisted development. You use AI code editors and agents (such as Cursor, Claude Code, Codex, or similar) every day, and you validate their output with tests, query results, and review before anything ships.

Clear written communication. You explain metric definitions, tradeoffs, and data caveats in plain language that Sales, RevOps, and Product partners understand.

BI and semantic layer experience. You can build dashboards and semantic models in Omni, Looker, or a similar BI tool, and you design metric definitions that people and AI tools can both query correctly.

Git-based workflows. You keep pull requests small and well described, and you give thoughtful code review.

Nice to have: Redshift performance tuning (sort and distribution keys), Python for scripting or API pulls, reverse ETL, and experience with product event, billing, payments, or ecommerce data.

Around 7 or more years in analytics engineering or data engineering where you personally shipped production data models.

Several years of hands-on work building and maintaining production dbt projects.

Working directly with sales teams and RevOps, and owning reporting they relied on to run the business.

Owning a data domain over time and evolving its models as the business and its questions changed.

Working at a SaaS company, ideally one with both a self-serve and a sales-led motion. Commerce, payments, or creator economy experience is a plus, not a requirement.

Working on a small or remote team where you were trusted to make decisions independently.

Ownership and follow-through. You treat your data domains as yours. You notice problems before stakeholders do, close the loop on requests, and finish what you start.

Judgment. You match rigor to the decision at hand, know when a quick answer is enough and when a request should become a durable model, and say so clearly.

Flexibility. You're glad to step outside your primary area when another team needs help, and you balance that work against your core commitments openly.

Teach everything you know. You document as you go, pair generously, and help stakeholders understand the data well enough to use it on their own.

Work in public. You share progress, open questions, and decisions in writing where the team can see them, including what didn't work.

Creator success is our success. You remember who's on the other end. A wrong number in a pipeline review means Sales spends time on the wrong creators, and that shapes how carefully you work.

Do it with excellence. You test your work, review it carefully, and don't ship a number you can't defend.

Candor with care. You're comfortable pushing back when a request won't answer the real question, and you do it in a way that builds trust.

Needing detailed specs or a highly structured environment before you can start.

Discomfort with ambiguity. Some of our sales and commerce definitions are still evolving, and you'll help settle them.

Needing several layers of approval to make modeling decisions within your domain.

Wanting to work only on dashboards, or only on pipelines, rather than across the transformation and BI layers.

Wanting a strictly fixed scope. Sales comes first, but you'll pick up Product or other work when the business needs it.

Wanting mostly greenfield work. Much of this role is improving sales models and dashboards that already exist.

Preferring to take tickets as written rather than partnering with stakeholders on what they need.

Being skeptical of AI's capabilities, or using AI without good judgment and accountability.

Expecting a large data team with dedicated specialists for every part of the stack.

We're 100% remote with team members across the globe. We work mostly on East Coast time - so if you're based on the West Coast of the United States, Europe, or Asia, you might have a few early mornings or late evenings. We do our best to keep things reasonable, but a little time zone juggling comes with the territory.

Kit has standardized salaries based on position, no matter where you live. For this role, we're hiring at our level 4 ($172,000). Level is determined based on experience and our interview process.

We cover up to $2,280 per month toward medical premiums, with dental and vision premiums fully covered. We offer Health Insurance plans through Aetna

$2,000 equipment allowance for your first two years, $1,000 budget every following two years. Company-provided laptops are issued to every Kit team member and are not included in the equipment budget

Individual learning + development budget ($3,500/year)

Four-week, paid sabbatical after five years with the team

#7132. Product Manager - Postgres at Supabase

Supabase is the Postgres development platform, built by developers for developers. We provide a complete backend solution including Database, Auth, Storage, Edge Functions, Realtime, and Vector Search. All services are deeply integrated and designed for growth.

We're hiring a Product Manager to own Postgres itself at Supabase. Every Supabase project is a full Postgres database, and Postgres is the foundation that Auth, Storage, Realtime and Edge Functions run on. You'll own the product experience of the database: Postgres versions and upgrades, maintenance, observability, extensions and security.

You'll work closely with our Postgres engineering teams and with the PM lead for Databases, who leads Multigres. You'll define the Postgres requirements that architecture must meet, and own how customers experience their database through that transition.

This role is ideal for someone who loves Postgres, sweats the details that make a database trustworthy, and is excited about improving the experience for millions of developers. Small improvements here compound across every project on the platform.

You'll own Postgres versions, upgrades and end-of-life. Which major and minor versions we support, how and when customers upgrade, and how we retire old versions. Your goal is to make upgrades routine rather than risky.

You'll make maintenance predictable. You'll design how we schedule, communicate and run maintenance windows so customers stay informed and in control, and disruption is rare and expected.

You'll help developers understand their database. Query performance, metrics, logs and advisors: you'll own the path from "my database is slow" to the fix.

You'll own our extension ecosystem. Which extensions we offer, how we evaluate and support new ones, and how we keep them working across versions.

You'll own Postgres security. Roles and permissions, Row Level Security ergonomics, secure defaults, and how we respond to Postgres and extension CVEs.

You'll talk to developers across the full spectrum, from indie developers on their first project to enterprises running critical workloads, and bring what you learn into every prioritization call.

You'll own the problem definition, requirements and success metrics for what we ship, such as upgrade success rate, the share of the fleet on supported versions, and time-to-diagnose for performance problems. You'll set the metric before the team builds, then track it after launch.

You'll keep Engineering, Support and GTM aligned. You'll own the plan of record for what's coming, why, and in what order, and communicate version changes and deprecations clearly to customers.

4+ years of Product Management experience on databases, developer tools or infrastructure, or equivalent depth as an engineer, DBA or founder moving into product. You're comfortable commenting on an architecture doc and advocating for the user when trade-offs are required.

Know Postgres well, or another relational database well enough to ramp quickly. You're comfortable reading EXPLAIN output and talking about replication, WAL, roles and extensions.

Have shipped products where reliability and trust are the experience. You treat downtime and breaking changes as product problems, not just operational ones.

Write clearly. Your specs, announcements and deprecation notices leave little room for confusion.

Build with what you own. You run queries, test upgrades and try extensions yourself, and form opinions through first-hand use.

Use AI to move faster. You use AI tools to compress research, drafting and feedback synthesis so you spend more time on judgment calls.

Work async by default. Supabase is fully remote. You make decisions in writing and move work forward without meetings.

Ran Postgres in production: upgrades, tuning, incident response.

Worked on database observability, performance tooling, or security and compliance products.

Contributed to Postgres, its extensions, or the wider open-source community.

We hire globally. We believe you can do your best work from anywhere. There are no Supabase offices, but we provide a WeWork membership or co-working allowance you can use anywhere in the world.
